Katalog Berita (Halaman 43/57)

#2921. Jim Cramer warns investors may be reading too much into Wall Street's post-election rally

CNBC's Jim Cramer warned Wednesday that Wall Street may be getting ahead of itself following the midterm elections.

Stocks rallied after Democrats scored a decisive victory in the midterms, but the gains masked a divide within technology. Hyperscalers surged as investors anticipated that greater opposition to data center development could slow the AI buildout and ease spending pressures. Meanwhile, semiconductor stocks and data center developers came under pressure. Cramer cautioned that investors may be drawing premature conclusions about the election's economic implications.

"The midterm results controlled today's trading, but by Friday we will be talking the Labor Department's nonfarm payrolls report, and we could see a repeal of so much of this move that I hesitate to guess where we go next," the "Mad Money" host said.

"But I do like the word guess," he added. "Because that sums up what I saw throughout today's session and it's not the stuff I want to base investment decisions on, even as it's terrific for a one-day trade for the ages."

Apple shares hit an all-time high Wednesday as investors bet that a slowdown in data center construction could ease memory chip prices, which have been pressuring the company's margins. Microsoft and Amazon also rallied on expectations that a slower AI buildout could reduce data center costs and rein in their massive capital spending.

Conversely, investors sold shares of companies whose growth depends on continued AI infrastructure investment, including chipmakers Nvidia and AMD and data center developers CoreWeave, Nebius and Iren.

But Cramer questioned whether a single election could fundamentally alter the forces driving the market. "I want you to understand that, as important as a midterm election can be, and this one is important, the elections did not upend the business cycle," Cramer said.

Cramer noted that he's aligned with JPMorgan strategist Michael Cembalest, who publishes the closely followed Eye on the Market commentary.

In his latest piece Wednesday, Cembalest wrote, "The business cycle drives markets, and the important components are things that midterms do not affect: 10 yr interest rates, commodity prices, Fed policy, corporate profits, budget deficits, defense spending, the pace of AI investment over the next 12 months, the ferocious borrowing needs of AI companies and the impact on credit spreads, etc."

Cramer said it's important that investors understand that short-term market moves may not always align with the actual fundamentals. "Never confuse what happens in the stock market with what happens in reality," he said.

Cramer's Charitable Trust, the portfolio run by CNBC's Investing Club, owns shares of AAPL, AMZN, MSFT, and NVDA.

#2919. Next step? Fischer says Nets hope for big return for vets

The Nets rebuild can probably be traced back to June 25, 2024 when Brooklyn made two trades within minutes of each other that signals half steps were over. One sent Mikal Bridges to New York for five first rounders, a first round swap and a second rounder; the other an exchange of firsts with the Rockets that brought back a first rounder and a first round swap they had originally sent Houston in the first James Harden trade.

The second step was the two-year-long tank that helped the Nets get two high — although not as high as they had hoped — firsts in Egor Demin and Mikel Brown Jr. as well as five other late firsts. The hope, at least among fans, was that the losing or at least the deliberate losing would be over. Not yet, apparently, as Brooklyn is off to a 1-6 start and the two rookies have shared the court for only a few minutes. For the second straight year, the Nets are also the youngest team in the league, not a recipe for contention.

Now, into the fan caldron of disappointment, Jake Fischer seemingly dropped some knowledge that would seemingly ensure a longer rebuild. Speaking to Yahoo! Sports, the writer suggested that the next step could be trying to move veterans Michael Porter Jr and/or Julius Randle presumably at the February trade deadline, presumably to a contender, for more assets, presumably more picks or maybe other assets. Scoring, already an issue, and wins would be sacrificed.

"Brooklyn, I would still categorize as a team that is in more of a sell situation. I believe that Brooklyn will hope that a Julius Randle, a Michael Porter Jr. could still potentially draw some real returns," Fischer said.

"The Nets, they don't have their draft pick this year and they're looking to be better and compete in the Eastern Conference, take some strides forward in the East.

"I don't think they're going to be a full sell job right now, but I do think that they are still in a position to solicit trade offers for their veterans to get draft capital, potentially young pieces back in return."

Reading carefully, rather than scanning aggregators' headlines, reveals that Fischer isn't making predictions that the Nets will push hard to move both. He talks about "a Julius Randle," "a Michael Porter Jr." not both. Nor does he specifically mention the trade deadline. Nor does he gauge interest levels on the other side.

Adding to the Nets' cache of assets, of course, does make sense from a number of aspects. If, as Sean Marks has said, the ultimate goal is a player or players who will "automatically and absolutely change the trajectory of the franchise." then the accumulation of assets makes sense, particularly in an era where trades, not free agency, is the route to acquiring stars and superstars. Look at all the stars who moved this summer. Only LeBron James signed as a free agent and that of course was an oddity.

Either of the two players would seem to be the ideal final piece for teams hoping to make a final run at a title. Randle and MPJ can put up big numbers and have. Porter Jr. also has a ring. Yeah sure, both have baggage, but their ability to get buckets is undisputed. As Basket News noted,

Porter Jr. is averaging 20.7 points, 7.0 rebounds, 1.4 assists and 1.6 steals, shooting 38.1% from the three-point line.

In the meantime, Randle holds the averages of 20.9 points, 7.7 rebounds and 4.9 assists.

Their contracts also militate for moves unless the Nets see them as part of their future. MPJ will be an unrestricted free agent come July. If the Nets don't plan on extending him — and there's no indication at this point that they do, then trading him for assets in the interim would be imperative. Similarly, Randle has a player option at the end of this season and he too can leave in July if Sean Marks & co. decide not to make him an offer or an offer he believes fair. (MPJ earns $40.8 million, Randle $33.3 million with an option of $35.8 million.)

Of the two, Porter Jr. would seem to be the more likely to return assets. He's nearly four years younger than Randle, although Randle is closer to iron man status, having averaged 68 games a year over the last eight. He also has proven that he can be a leader during his time at Flatbush and Atlantic.

It should also be noted that any trade is unlikely to produce the automatic and absolute game-changer Marks has spoken about. More likely, it'd be the big trade before the bigger trade which might happen in the summer of 2027.

One of the most intriguing but also less than concrete suggestions in Fischer's comment is that the Nets might move away from accumulating draft picks to seeking "potentially young pieces" — that is, undervalued players on rookie deals. After all, they do have 13 firsts and 19 seconds over the next seven years. That strategy has been a key element in a number of recent rebuilds, most notably OKC's acquisition of a young Shai Gilgeous-Alexander, in the trade of Paul George to the Clippers back in June 2019. That one trade got Sam Presti one building block after another from SGA to Aday Mara. We should be so lucky.

Of course the trade deadline — 3:00 p.m. ET on February 11 — is three months away and there are few trades in the first couple of months of any season. The unofficial start of trade season is December 15 for two reasons. It's roughly 20 games into the season when teams understand what their needs are going forward as well as the first day that players signed or resigned during the off-season can be dealt. That expands the pool of players who are available by dozens. That's especially relevant putting together complicated, multi-team deals. Perhaps the spate of early season injuries will move things up, but it's unlikely.

#2917. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2913. What Republicans' huge loss means for Trump

Democrats just put President Donald Trump in a bind.

The party is projected to win commanding majorities in the House and Senate after thrashing Republicans in key races up and down the ballot across the country. The blue tsunami will knock Washington on its head and crush the Republican congressional bulwark that both supported and shielded Trump through the first two years of his term.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The midterm result snuffs out any chance Trump had of passing partisan legislation in the second half of his presidency. And many of the interests he has been able to pursue without Congress' input — including tariffs, the Iran war and an array of Washington-area construction projects — are sure to face sudden pushback from newly empowered Democratic majorities.

The paradigm shift hardly leaves Trump helpless, however. As president at a time of executive power expansion, Trump wields singular authorities that could stymie Democrats' efforts to pursue their own agendas.

But with his grip on the Capitol shriveling and his approval ratings scraping record lows in numerous polls, it's unclear how much support he'll be able to muster to fight the coming political battles.

Even before Election Day, analysts and pundits had contemplated a Democratic rout so decisive that it would force a reckoning for the GOP, and potentially even pose an existential threat to Trump's "Make America Great Again" movement.

Marc Short, chairman of Advancing American Freedom and onetime chief of staff to former Vice President Mike Pence, told CNBC on Tuesday afternoon that the GOP has moved away from its conservative foundations.

"We need a refresh right now," Short said in an interview. "We've lost our way."

Trump has been vague about what he hopes to achieve with the rest of his term. But he has shared a vision of how he might coexist with a Democratic majority, and it involves heavy use of the veto pen.

"It's one of the stronger powers that a president has," Trump said in a Fox News interview overnight. "They'll send over things that are insane, and I'll veto them."

"And after a couple of months of vetoes, they'll call and they'll say, 'How do we get this stuff done?' And we'll work together," he said, adding, "I have a lot of friends that are Democrats."

Trump has previously stated his willingness to veto reams of legislation that Democrats send across his desk. In a New York Post podcast interview released Monday, Trump said that if Republicans don't win, "I veto everything."

Pressed to detail his plans for the next two years, Trump touted his record and said, "The most important thing is to keep it going. It's going to get better and better and better, unless people come in that hate our country."

If that happens, "I guess I'll become Mr. Veto," Trump said.

He may soon get his chance: House Democrats, poised to be led by current Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York, are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned.

The legislative push, first reported by The New York Times, aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to introduce bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law earlier this year.

Democrats also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the war with Iran, or seek congressional authorization to keep it going.

But without Trump's approval, those bills may end up as little more than symbolic gestures, as Democrats will lack the two-thirds majorities in both chambers that are required to override a presidential veto without GOP help.

And there's no guarantee the bills will even make it out of Congress: Despite their landslide victories, Senate Democrats will still end up shy of the 60 votes required to bypass the chamber's filibuster rules for most legislation, giving Republicans key leverage in the minority.

Democrats also could feasibly benefit from Trump's vetoes in the long term. Forcing the president to shoot down numerous popular bills could give them fodder in the 2028 election.

It's far from clear whether Trump believes he can find compromise with Democrats on any of their initial legislative priorities.

But any potential for a working relationship with the opposition could be poisoned early on, as Democrats in both chambers are aggressively signaling their plans to launch politically charged investigations against Trump, his political and corporate allies and even his family members.

Winning control of House committees gives Democrats the ability to issue subpoenas under the current rules.

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., who is likely to chair the House Oversight Committee in the next Congress, said his panel will target the Trump family's business dealings, corporate corruption and alleged abuses by federal immigration agencies. He also plans to return focus to the Trump administration's handling of files related to notorious sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, Garcia summed up his plans in a two-word statement: "Lawyer up."

It's not just Garcia. Other Democrats have already demanded records from the Trump administration on a range of issues. Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., for instance, last week accused Trump of using tariff exemptions to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

Senate Democrats have sent similar signals.

"They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we'll be printing," Sen. Ed Markey, D-Mass., said in an X post late Tuesday in response to a post, appearing to refer to tech executives.

The investigations are widely expected to consume much of the rest of Trump's term.

Trump has ample experience being targeted by Democrats, who impeached him twice in the House during his first presidential term.

But with his base of support appearing to erode inside and outside of Washington, Trump may have to face his political foes with a smaller arsenal than he's used to.

"I'm not expecting Republicans to revolt" against Trump following the midterm losses, AAF's Short told CNBC. "But I'm expecting some of them to quietly distance themselves."

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

Here are the latest news updates and possible return dates for Knicks players...

The Knicks have ruled Josh Hart out for the team's game against the Bulls on Wednesday due to right ankle soreness he suffered in the team's win against the Nets on Monday.

This is the first game Hart will miss this season and the second Knick to not be available due to an injury after bench guard Jose Alvarado missed four games with an ankle injury.

Following Wednesday's game, the Knicks have a few days off before New York heads to Cleveland to take on the Cavaliers on Saturday night.

The Knicks are listing Josh Hart as questionable for Wednesday's game against the Chicago Bulls due to right ankle soreness.

Hart has played in all seven of New York's games this season, averaging 8.6 points, 7.3 rebounds, and 3.9 assists over 31.7 minutes per contest.

Additionally, Chicago (1-6) is listing big men Nic Claxton (hamstring) as questionable and Jalen Smith (left finger sprain) as probable for the game.

#2902. Rams cornerback Jaylen Watson to miss rest of season after shoulder surgery

Rams cornerback Jaylen Watson will have shoulder surgery and is out for the season, coach Sean McVay said Wednesday.

Watson, who signed with the Rams as a free agent after winning two Super Bowl titles in four seasons with the Kansas City Chiefs, reinjured his shoulder during last Sunday's victory over the Chargers at SoFi Stadium.

"That's a big blow," McVay said. "Bummer for him. He's been such a stud for us. He wanted to be able to play through it but just kind of understanding the totality of it all, just some of the different things, recommended to get that surgery.

"He's been everything and that much more that we had hoped."

Watson, who signed a three-year contract that included $34 million in guarantees, has one interception and nine passes defended.

"A guy that will be missed for sure," quarterback Matthew Stafford said. "He's a big-time talent and a guy that was kind of an enforcer for us on the edge."

Read more: Myles Garrett returning to Rams practice, but it's unclear when he'll play

Josh Wallace and Emmanuel Forbes Jr. are expected to help fill Watson's spot, and McVay indicated that the Rams could explore adding a cornerback before Tuesday's NFL trade deadline.

McDuffie, who has intercepted two passes, suffered a back injury against the Chargers and was not scheduled to practice on Wednesday.

The Rams opened the window for edge rusher Myles Garrett, tight end Terrance Ferguson and running back Ronnie Rivers to return from injured reserve, and all were scheduled to practice Wednesday.

Stafford downplayed having left the locker room on Sunday with an ice pack on his right elbow. He said he has iced throughout the season. "I'm 38 years old, trying to keep up with these young bucks," he said. "Little bit of soreness and stuff that is not uncommon this time of year. Again, haven't a rep in practice. Haven't missed a rep in a game. Feel good. So I'm not too worried about it." … The Rams cut receiver/punt returner Alex Bachman and re-signed him to the practice squad. McVay said running back Kyren Williams or receiver Tutu Atwell would return punts. The Rams also signed cornerback Cam Lampkin to the roster from the practice squad, added cornerback Nyzier Fourqurean to the practice squad and cut outside linebacker Garrett Nelson. Running back Dean Connors was placed on injured reserve after undergoing surgery for a hand injury suffered against the Chargers.

#2899. For today and tomorrow, Labaron Philion needs more minutes

The Sixers are 7-1 after Monday night's 126-107 victory against Chicago. Three of their next four games are against fellow Eastern Conference contenders in Detroit, Cleveland and Boston. Perhaps for that reason, Nick Nurse might not make drastic changes to a rotation that keeps winning. After all, the Sixers haven't opened a playoff round at home since 2023 so locking up wins against other teams in the Eastern Conference now can only help for seeding later. However, there's one glaring mistake with Nurse's rotation that he needs to address.

When it comes to the bench, Nurse is playing Anfernee Simons, Dean Wade and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope regularly. These are veterans who figure to factor into the playoff rotation as well come the spring. However, whether Labaron Philon sees the court in April and beyond or not, the rookie out of Alabama should be seeing more than the eight minutes per game he's currently averaging. Even in a 19-point win against the Bulls, Philon played just two minutes.

While Philon was drafted towards the end of the first round this past summer, now's the time to find out what kind of player he could become. Of course, he can always grow into a bigger role next season and beyond, but should the Sixers set him on a path that's essentially burning a year of his rookie contract? Maybe that's a harsh way of phrasing things after only eight games, but it's the realest way of saying Philon isn't playing nearly enough.

For context, Tyrese Maxey was drafted 21st overall in 2020 which is just one spot earlier than Philon's 22nd overall in 2026. In the 2020-21 season, Maxey's rookie year, Maxey played 15 minutes per game and started eight games. That's a considerable uptick from the amount of minutes Philon is currently playing. Additionally, under Nick Nurse in 2024-25, Jared McCain played 25.7 minutes per game as a rookie in an injury-shortened season that saw him still start eight games. Neither of these comparisons is perfect as McCain was nearly a lottery pick having been selected 15th overall in 2024 and Maxey would have probably been a lottery pick under non-COVID circumstances in his draft eligible season in college.

Part of the reason we're saying the Sixers have a backup center problem yet again is because they whiffed on the Johni Broome pick recently and Adem Bona doesn't seem to have any offensive skillset. Granted, those two players were recent second-rounders by Philadelphia, but the point stands. You want to get your draft picks on the court in their rookie seasons in the regular season when your rotation should be deeper. It's cheap production if they're good right away, gives you more clarity on what they could develop into down the line, and would also make it easier to move off of them in trades while they still have some trade value if the early returns aren't to your liking.

In the short term, don't we all agree Maxey could use more of a breather than what he's currently getting? Maxey is averaging a whopping 37.4 minutes per game which is currently second in the NBA to only Denver's Jamal Murray. Maxey's injury history is nowhere near as lengthy as Joel Embiid's, but that doesn't mean Maxey wouldn't benefit from seeing his minutes scaled back a bit. Maxey's also doesn't seem like the kind of player that would be concerned about various individual accolades that might not come his way if his playing time decreased. More importantly, everyone can agree Maxey needs to be healthy and full go come playoff time for Philadelphia to make a deep run.

In the long term, wouldn't it be great if Philon developed into a sixth man at the very least for this team? Remember how awful Philly's bench was in the playoffs last year? Philon represents a long-term solution to what's been a long-term problem for the Sixers even if it's hard to see him ever getting on the court with long-term building blocks in Maxey, VJ Edgecombe and Jaylen Brown all at the same time.

Make no mistake, winning is fun and the Sixers are doing a lot of it right now. But the best teams in every sport can fuse the present with the future and win in the current season and in seasons of tomorrow. I get it Sixers fans, the current season is probably all that's on your mind and I can't blame you as the Sixers have as good a chance as any other team does to win the NBA championship in 2026-27. But there's going to be basketball played in 2027 and beyond and Labaron Philon was drafted to be a part of that basketball for Philly so this season should also be about finding out what role Philon can have within this organization's long-term plans.

#2896. Analysis: The Democrats have one chance to extract concessions from Trump. Markets will hate it

The electoral sweep by Democrats on Tuesday night injects a new, unstable dynamic into the single biggest political risk to financial markets in the coming year: the debt limit. Unless a defeated Republican Party makes a swift and unlikely show of unity to take the issue off the table in its last few weeks in power, the protracted fight that will unfold is set to be nastier than ever, adding volatility to an already shaky bond market.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

Past episodes of brinkmanship around the need to raise the government's borrowing limit have involved Republicans in Congress demanding budget cuts from a Democratic president as the price of their vote. This time it will almost certainly be the left seeking concessions from the right.

The debt limit is the definition of a must-pass bill, even more than issues like funding the government and the defense budget. That makes it potentially the one real chance the new Democratic majority in Congress has to extract concessions from a president who is already pledging to veto their agenda. Markets will hate the entire process, even if — as many expect — it ends in compromise.

Because it is Democrats who hold the leverage, unlike past episodes, the ultimate agreement is more likely to increase government spending than lower it. With high bond yields weighing on the economy, and Americans expecting action from a newly empowered Democratic opposition, the fight could be particularly damaging.

Many in the markets are stuck on the outdated idea that Democrats are less assertive than Republicans, said Aniket Shah, head of the Washington policy team at the investment bank Jefferies.

"They have this sort of mental image of Biden-Harris 2024," said Shah. "I don't think they realize how aggressively Democrats plan to take 2027."

The debt ceiling is a quirk of the congressional budgeting process. It stops the government from borrowing funds that Congress has already ordered federal agencies to spend. And because Congress has run deficits for decades — this year at $1.99 trillion — the pile of debt periodically bumps into the debt ceiling.

Congress last set the limit in 2025, when it gave the Treasury Department the authority to borrow $41.1 trillion. The Treasury is likely to hit its maximum borrowing capacity by midsummer, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonprofit that tracks the issue.

The Treasury can use budgeting techniques to extend the so-called X-date, when the U.S. would no longer be able to meet all its obligations, to sometime in the fourth quarter of 2027, according to Wall Street estimates.

A few technical hiccups aside, the U.S. has never meaningfully breached the debt limit. The results of the U.S. missing payments would be chaotic as damage ripples out across the financial system into the global economy. U.S. default would immediately call into question trillions in Treasury securities that are the lifeblood of the global financial system. Damaging them could send the U.S. and global economies into an economic tailspin with no obvious means of recovery.

Because the stakes are so high, Shah and many others on Wall Street have come to believe that the episode will produce volatility but will ultimately be resolved in compromise.

Fights under former Presidents Barack Obama and Joe Biden saw Republican congressional majorities use the issue to ram through spending reductions. (The 2025 raise was less contentious, because Republicans held Congress and the presidency. It was passed along party lines.)

Still, the risk-taking in those episodes contributed to ratings firms' decisions to downgrade U.S. debt — and could again.

President Donald Trump has called for Republicans to resolve the issue in the next few weeks, before the new Democratic-majority Congress is seated.

That isn't likely. The 2025 debt limit increase only passed because it was tied to a package of tax cuts and other policy changes. Republican debt hawks complained but held their noses.

Republicans may attempt to raise the limit in the coming lame-duck session under a budgeting procedure called reconciliation, which would allow them to move ahead without Democrats' votes. But success would require unity among a fractured, fleeting Republican majority that also faces several other more pressing priorities.

The most likely path toward raising the debt limit is the incoming Democratic majority negotiating another temporary fix with Trump. But they aren't going to make it easy. The president has occasionally made noises about making deals, but the political incentives ahead of the 2028 presidential election are for both sides to dig in.

"[Democrats] are going to want to embarrass the White House, and they're going to want to get leverage," Shah said. Democrats will use that to push to reverse cuts to Medicaid spending passed in 2025 and to try to restore healthcare spending, he said.

Democrats could also seek to tie the debt limit to restraining signature Trump policies, such as limiting the war with Iran or rolling back tariffs, analysts at Goldman Sachs said in a research note Wednesday.

Another potential compromise being considered in some corners of Washington is for Democrats to tie an increase in the corporate tax rate to a debt limit raise, said George Pollack, senior U.S. analyst with Signum Global Advisors. Trump has shown he can be surprisingly flexible on similar issues, Pollack noted.

That is what will make this debt limit fight different. In the past, Republicans used it to try to win spending reductions they couldn't get through regular budget negotiations. Now Democrats will broaden it out into an all-purpose hostage. That effort is far more likely to raise overall government spending than cut it. "There's a very weird irony here," said Robert Sockin, chief U.S. economist at PGIM, an asset manager.

The path to compromise will likely be rocky. Sockin believes it may turn out like the 2025 government shutdown. That was the longest government-wide shutdown in history, even though Democrats weren't in control of either chamber of Congress. This time they will have even more power.

If the fight extends beyond the X-date, missing debt payments could do serious damage to the financial system.

"It's not our base case, but we think the risk is higher than ever that you could get a technical default from the government," Sockin said. That would see the U.S. miss a payment, even if the government eventually makes it up.

That would still hurt. "You'll get more downgrades to the U.S. sovereign, and those might bite more this time," Sockin said. Yields on longer-term Treasurys would rise to account for added uncertainty.

Crossing the red line could prompt a "very negative reaction in equity markets," he said.

The coming months will show just how close Democrats are willing to get to that line.

#2893. NFL Injury Report, Week 9: Latest news, updates on Bhayshul Tuten, Breece Hall, more players for fantasy football

Week 9 is the official midway point of the 2026 NFL season. So by now you know what you have on your fantasy football roster. You also know who has been healthy the majority of the year and which players have missed time. The injuries keep coming as we get into the middle of the campaign. As always, we're tracking all the latest news and updates for you, to help with those fantasy football lineups.

Lamar Jackson, BAL (thumb) — Will be placed on injured reserve

Tyler Huntley, BAL (toe) — Reportedly done for the season

Deshaun Watson, CLE (concussion protocol)

Ravens QBs: Jackson left in the first half of Sunday's loss to the Bills. He needs surgery on his thumb and is headed for IR. He'll miss at least four games, if not the rest of the season. After Jackson exited, Huntley entered the game and also had to leave due to injury (turf toe), which will end his season. That leaves Baltimore down to third-string rookie Joe Fagnano, who will start against Jacksonville on TNF. Hayden Winks takes a closer look at what Fagnano could bring. The Ravens traded for Jacoby Brissett, but he will not be ready to play this week.

The QB instability is obviously a huge downgrade to the passing game. Fagnano went 10 of 21 for 91 yards and an INT in his first NFL action in Week 8. Zay Flowers is more of a WR3/flex option while Derrick Henry should get a healthy amount of work, though in a tough matchup. Outside of those two, the rest of the Ravens offense should be avoided in fantasy until we see more from Fagnano or the Ravens make a deal for a QB.

Darnold: The Seahawks will be without Darnold in Week 9 against the Cardinals due to quad and rib injuries. With Darnold out, the Seahawks plan to start Drew Lock … who is also dealing with an elbow injury. Lock was excellent when he had to start in place of Darnold against Arizona in Week 2, going 19 of 26 for 235 yards and three touchdowns in a 31-7 win. Jaxon Smith-Njigba caught nine passes for 155 yards and three touchdowns in that game, proving JSN is QB-proof and an auto-start again this week.

Bhayshul Tuten, JAX (ankle/foot) — Game-time decision

Jeremiyah Love, ARI (ankle) — Will be placed on IR, miss 4-5 weeks

Breece Hall, NYJ (quad) — Expected to be back in Week 9

Travis Etienne Jr., NO (hamstring) — Could be activated off IR

Tuten: Jaguars coach Liam Coen said the running back will be a game-time decision for Thursday's game against the Ravens. Tuten was a DNP all three days to start the week, with ankle and foot issues. He is coming off his best game of the season. He took 18 touches for 159 yards and two scores in Week 8. Chris Rodriguez Jr. is also dealing with a hand injury but was limited on Monday and Tuesday.

If Tuten is out, Rodriguez gets elevated into the RB2/3 conversation given the Jags are a 10-point favorite. In the rout of the Colts last week, Rodriguez had 58 yards on seven touches and a score. Ameer Abdullah also saw six carries against Indy and could see some work if Tuten is sidelined.

Hall: We thought the Jets were going to get back Hall in Week 8 against the Raiders. That wasn't the case and we got another solid game from Braelon Allen. At this point, even if Hall comes back, expect Allen to be involved given how well he's performed. Allen is the RB5 overall in half-PPR scoring from Weeks 5-8 with Hall sidelined. We'll need to get a look at how the split is in Week 9 with Hall, who got in a limited practice Wednesday, expected back. Plus, it's a tough draw against Kansas City's run defense. But better matchups loom, so don't be surprised if both Hall and Allen are viable RB2/3 options.

Cardinals RBs: Love suffered a new high-ankle sprain in the comeback win over Dallas in OT on Sunday. Knight exited after a big hit and should land in concussion protocol. It'll be tough for Knight to clear in time for Week 9 against the Seahawks, but we'll see. Love has had so many issues with the ankle in his rookie season, and is expected to miss 4-5 weeks while on injured reserve, per NFL Network's Ian Rapoport. With Love already out, if Knight sits, Tyler Allgeier will be playable, but don't expect 33.6 fantasy points — or anywhere close to that — against Seattle's top defense.

But with Love on IR and Knight starting the season with a lesser role, expect Allgeier to be the lead back moving forward. There could be weeks when Knight is healthy in which the staff rides the hot hand. But Allgeier was brought in to be a high-value backup to Love. Allgeier will have RB2/3 value week-to-week depending on the matchup. Also keep an eye out for James Conner, who is on IR but could be a useful stash.

McCaffrey: CMC comes off the bye navigating a mild hamstring strain. It doesn't feel like this will cause him to miss Week 9 against the Raiders. But we saw before San Francisco's bye that Kyle Shanahan feels comfortable giving the ball to rookie Kaelon Black. We could see more of Black on Sunday to help keep McCaffrey fresh.

Garrett Wilson, NYJ (concussion protocol)

Emeka Egbuka, TB (foot) — Could play in Week 9

Jordyn Tyson, NO (hamstring) — Could come off IR

Watson: The Packers' lead receiver had a miraculous exit and return to TNF's win over the Panthers in Week 8. Watson finished with an 8-113-0 line on 10 targets, catching a few passes after coming back from the hospital. But Watson revealed he's dealing with a broken rib, which is less than ideal. #2891. Dumont family takes full control of Mavericks, buying Miriam Adelson's shares

Patrick and Sivan Dumont have formally become the controlling owners of the Dallas Mavericks after purchasing Miriam Adelson's shares of the franchise last month, a story broken by Tim MacMahon of ESPN. The Dumonts now own 69% of the Mavericks.

While this is a change at the top, it should have little day-to-day impact, as Patrick Dumont has served as team governor and been the acting owner on the ground since the day Mark Cuban sold the team to the Dumonts and Adelsons. Here is what Dumont told ESPN:

"It's really about long-term investment in the city of Dallas," Dumont told ESPN on Tuesday. "The Dumont family is really committed to building what we hope is the best arena in the NBA, a really significant investment in the city that will help create the new 40-year home for the Dallas Mavericks, a great experience for our fans and inspire our team to win championships. That's really what we're focused on. We had the opportunity, and we're really excited about it."

As Cuban said when he sold his majority share of the team, the NBA has become a real estate business, and what Dumont is looking to build is not just a new arena but the anchor of a much larger development. That is the trend with professional sports franchises in general, including the Dallas Cowboys.

Dumont, the Las Vegas Sands Co. CEO, has experience with building and development. He didn't have experience as an NBA owner when he took over the team, and that position comes with a learning curve (a longer one for some than others). Dumont became a villain early in his ownership of the Mavericks when he approved the controversial trade of Luka Doncic to the Lakers, which led to severe backlash from fans in Dallas. Dumont told ESPN's MacMahon he learned from those experiences.

"The most important thing every day is just trying to work harder to get better," Dumont said. "It's something that really applies in life, but it's something that really applies to here. It's been an experience as a new owner in the league. Dallas is a great sports city. I think we have the best fans in the NBA, very passionate, and it's my job to make sure that we win and that we support our fans. I'm really focused on that. I've learned that there are a lot of things you can do -- sometimes they work, sometimes they don't -- but you've got to keep pushing, got to keep going."

Dumont made changes in the front office, bringing in Masai Ujiri to run the show, then Ujiri fired coach Jason Kidd to bring in Dusty May. Dumont spent millions to upgrade the team's practice facilities. All of this with the idea of trying to maximize the team around Cooper Flagg, who in his second NBA season is looking elite and like an All-NBA player already. The new arena will ultimately be remembered as the one Cooper built.

#2889. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2888. Inside India newsletter: India weighs a tax cut to win over foreign investors. Will it work?

Hello, this is Priyanka Salve, writing to you from Singapore.

Welcome to the latest edition of "Inside India" — your one-stop destination for stories and developments from the world's fastest-growing large economy.

Once a favorite emerging market for foreign investors, Indian equities have been losing their charm. Foreign holdings of Indian stocks are currently at an all-time low of 14%. Now a tax cut, which puts India on par with several Asian markets, is being proposed to win back these investors.

This week, I spoke with experts to find out whether a tax cut alone can restore the Indian stock market's appeal to foreign investors.

Any thoughts on today's newsletter? Share them with the team.

The clamor for removing tax on long-term capital gains, or LTCG, on foreign portfolio investors is rising in India as the country's equity markets witness record capital outflows.

On Monday, Reuters reported that India's capital market regulator has urged the government to scrap the LTCG tax for foreign investors.

One of India's oldest and most prominent industry bodies, PHD Chamber of Commerce and Industry, last week asked the government appealing to abolish the tax.

It argued that the capital gains tax was increased in 2024 at a time when the stock market was "near record highs" and as the market conditions have now changed, the government needs to review it.

The country's Finance Minister Nirmala Sitharaman on Monday acknowledged the exit of foreign capital outflows and added she would consider the calls for reviewing LTCG tax, as per local media reports.

These demands come at a time when foreign investors' appetite for Indian equities has sharply declined. As per a Nomura report on Monday, foreign holdings in Indian stocks are at an all-time low of 14%.

Experts agree that the removal of the tax cut would improve foreign investors' sentiment by putting India's tax regime on par with other emerging markets. But the tax cut alone may not be enough to bring back foreign investors in droves, they said.

"A tax cut helps at the margin, but allocators tend to buy expected returns, not tax breaks," said Amit Bhartia, portfolio manager at Singapore-based Delorean Partners. He added that foreign investors have exited India as earnings growth became less predictable, while risks shot up.

Bhartia highlighted that foreign investors put in about 570 billion rupees ($6 billion) into Indian equities in September of 2024, and a month later pulled out 940 billion rupees to chase cheaper Chinese stocks. "The tax rate didn't change between those two months. The perceived relative return did," Bhartia explained.

Until the record exit in March this year, the outflows in October 2024 were the highest in a month.

Multiple risks are weighing down Indian markets right now, including strained ties with the U.S., higher energy prices, a depreciating currency and uncertainty over the impact of artificial intelligence.

A possible LTCG cut for foreign investors, a drop in global bond yields and expectations of the rupee holding up on central bank intervention would lend support to the Indian markets, Nomura said in its report.

However, it added that from a longer-term perspective, concerns around geopolitical and technological uncertainty persist, "weighing on long-term growth expectations."

India was the world's third worst-performing market after Indonesia and Turkey between January and October this year, the report showed. India's benchmark Nifty 50 as lost nearly 9% so far this year.

Foreign investors, who just three years ago celebrated India as the best-performing emerging market, have soured over the last two years, cashing out net $18.9 billion in 2025 and more than $30 billion so far this year.

India has "to fight for capital in a competition it could once afford to take for granted," global brokerage Bernstein said in a report on Monday. It argued that multiple "catalysts," including a tax cut and easing of tensions in the Middle East, are needed to reverse capital outflows from India.

"India's valuations remain expensive, while earnings growth has been disappointing," the report said.

As per the data shared in the report, India is trading at a rolling 12-month forward price-to-earnings of 18.6 times, higher than Taiwan's 16.8 times and Korea's 3.9 times. Both these markets have been outperformers in the emerging countries, propped up by strong artificial intelligence names.

On the earnings growth front, while emerging markets on average are expected to deliver a 45% improvement, India's forecast is much slower at 11%, as per Bernstein.

The tax cut alone will not "trigger a major reallocation into Indian equities as the fundamental trade-off facing foreign investors remains unchanged," Hanna Luchnikava-Schorsch, head of Asia-Pacific economics at S&P Global Market Intelligence, told CNBC.

As cracks emerge in the global AI rally, data from LSEG shows Indian benchmark indexes have outperformed global peers over the past month, as some investors see the Indian market as a hedge against AI. Foreign capital flows at the start of this month have been positive. But will they sustain?

India has a strong structural growth story, but investors still need earnings growth to justify the valuation premium, Luchnikava-Schorsch said.

#2885. Jeremy Sochan’s fall from NBA lottery pick and champion to the G League

When the Spurs first drafted Jeremy Sochan out of Baylor with the 9th overall pick in 2022, I remember probably being more thrilled with the pick than warranted. I hardly knew anything about him, but I did know he had size and was not a guard, and that's all I wanted. If you ignore 2019 (when the Spurs drafted a bust power forward in Luka Samanic and a truly "small" forward in Keldon Johnson) and 2015 (when they drafted and stashed center Nikola Milutinov), you otherwise had to go all the way back to 2014 and Kyle Anderson to find the last time they drafted a player who was not a guard or small wing in the first round. (And even then, Anderson was more of a guard in a forward's body.)

As good as some of those picks were — steals with Dejounte Murray and Derrick White late in the first round, Devin Vassell as their first lottery pick since Tim Duncan — others were merely okay or even complete busts that failed to address what was constantly an area of weakness during the post-Kawhi Leonard era: size at the forward positions. They finally changed up their drafting pattern when they took Sochan, and he seemed like a great fit with the club, both by filling a position of need and bringing a sense of humor during a couple of rough "tanking" seasons.

He had a good rookie season with the Spurs, making the All-Rookie 2nd Team and proving to be one of the better perimeter defenders in the league. He also had a knack for defending the rim and grabbing rebounds despite being slightly undersized when playing center. Across his first three seasons, he averaged a consistent 11+ points and 6+ rebounds on solid shooting numbers — inside the arc. A good comparison for him would be Malik Rose (the Spurs' undersized backup big man from their first two championship teams) but therein lies the problem. Rose was ideal for that role in the 2000's, but a power forward/undersized center without a three-point shot just doesn't fit today's game, and that's where Sochan found himself.

After winning the lottery and drafting Victor Wembanyama in 2023, experimentation began to figure out how to best utilize them together. There was the ghastly Point Sochan experiment in his second season (Wemby's first), and he lost his starting spot and eventually fell out of the rotation while dealing with various injuries during his third season. Finally, by last season, it was clear he wasn't part of Mitch Johnson's future plans despite having good chemistry with Wemby. He requested a trade and was eventually waived and signed with the New York Knicks, where he didn't play a role but at least won a championship against his former team.

That would be the last bright moment in his NBA career that started promising but dimmed with time. He signed a training camp deal with the Portland Trail Blazers this summer, but despite a solid preseason, he was waved before the start of the regular season. He was then drafted 1st overall by the Texas Legends in the G League Draft (the Dallas Mavericks' affiliate), marking a stark fall from just a year ago.

At his introductory press conference, he was humble and respectful. When asked about the roller coaster of the last 10+ months, had the following to say:

"I think the way I grew up, my family, my parents; it's always been like that. I could look at it negatively (the Portland situation). But when I look back at that situation, I think the two months I was there, I grew a lot. I was healthy throughout the whole training camp, preseason and open gym. So I think that was a blessing. When we got into preseason games, the minutes I got I think I used them to the best of my ability. I had good games. So what would be the point of being negative and angry for getting waived? Of course, you can look at it as that's the NBA and I have a contract. But at the same time I have good momentum. So, why would I hold weight on something that I can't control? And instead, be in this moment and do what I can do and keep that momentum going."

He also discussed having to let go of the past and focus on what he's learned so he can move forward.

"I think the biggest lesson for me is just being as present as possible. I keep going with this 'presence' because there's a lot of things that you can't control — in life and in basketball. Sometimes I feel like I've been a victim of the past where I've kinda held onto that, instead of acknowledging that and the just keep going. I think that's the biggest lesson, for sure."

It has certainly been tough to watch how far the former fan favorite has fallen. Even if it was for the better, I was personally sad to see Sochan leave San Antonio (enough so that I didn't begrudge him for getting a ring with the Knicks). He mentions being a victim of the past, and while I'm not sure what his own context is, he is certainly a prime example of a player who would have been much better off in a bygone era of basketball than he is today.

Hopefully this isn't the end of his NBA journey. While it's odd to see a former NBA lottery pick with four years of experience enter into the G League draft, there are no experience limits, and he fit the requirement of having recently been waived from a training camp roster. Choosing the G League — as opposed to going overseas — shows that he is still very much interested in making it back to the NBA and is not ready to settle for anything less.

Hopefully his signature competitive drive and determination keeps him going, and he eventually makes it back someday. He was a fan favorite for a reason, and even if the parting was mutual, I think we can all agree that we want Sochan to get another chance in the NBA, even if it's not with the Spurs.

#2884. LIV Golf faces liquidation after BC Partners scraps rescue plan

LIV Golf could be headed for liquidation after BC Partners Credit reportedly dropped plans to finance a relaunch of the breakaway league.

The investment firm had pledged up to $300 million for a revamped version of LIV, known as LIV 2.0, but withdrew the plan after LIV Golf failed to secure enough player commitments, according to the Associated Press.

LIV is scheduled to appear in U.S. bankruptcy court Thursday, with the hearing now expected to focus on liquidation of its assets. The league's ultimate fate remains unclear.

LIV Golf and BC Partners did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

LIV filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection in September after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund said it would stop funding the league after the 2026 season.

PIF had invested more than $5 billion since LIV launched in 2022, according to its Chapter 11 filing.

BC Partners' proposed relaunch would have made players majority owners of the reorganized league and featured a scaled-back schedule.

But LIV has lost several prominent players, including Jon Rahm and Sergio Garcia, as it sought commitments for the new venture.

Rahm said Tuesday that uncertainty over the league's future and proposed changes to its format influenced his decision to leave.

"There were too many unknowns at the time, and there's still quite a few," he told reporters ahead of the Abu Dhabi Championship. Rahm added that the revamped competition appeared to be moving away from the team format that initially attracted him.

Garcia, who secured a release from his LIV contract in October, has outlined plans to return to the DP World Tour and explore a possible return to the PGA Tour.

Only four players had publicly committed to LIV 2.0: Richard Bland, Cameron Tringale, Brendan Steele and Ben Campbell, according to Golf Digest.

LIV launched in 2022 as a challenger to the PGA Tour, recruiting prominent players including Phil Mickelson, Dustin Johnson and Bryson DeChambeau with lucrative contracts.

Its arrival split men's professional golf, with the PGA Tour suspending members who competed in LIV events. The disputes later spilled into the courts, with players challenging suspensions and the rival organizations pursuing competing legal claims.

The PGA Tour denied the allegations, while LIV dismissed the counterclaims.

A June 2023 framework agreement between the PGA Tour, DP World Tour and PIF ended their litigation and proposed combining their commercial businesses, although the deal was never completed.

#2883. Inside the chip talent war: Trade secrets and rising Chinese rivals

A former Samsung Electronics employee who later joined Chinese memory chipmaker ChangXin Memory Technologies, or CXMT, was reportedly sentenced by the Seoul High Court on Oct. 22 to five years in prison for leaking Samsung's technology.

The sentence was reduced from seven years at his first trial in April. The former employee had joined CXMT in 2016.

The case highlights an intensifying battle over semiconductor know-how as Chinese chipmakers narrow the gap with established rivals, putting the race for top engineering talent increasingly at odds with efforts to protect trade secrets.

In 2023, a South Korean court granted Samsung's request to block a former DRAM researcher from joining U.S. rival Micron. It ruled that the two-year non-compete was not excessive and was necessary to protect Samsung's technology and business information, JoongAng Ilbo reported.

A decade ago, Chinese memory chipmakers were not Samsung's biggest concern when it came to losing experienced engineers.

Instead, the South Korean company was more focused on employees leaving for established rivals such as SK Hynix and Micron, according to a former employee who asked not to be identified because the information was private.

Samsung has been monitoring where certain departing employees went — to signal to remaining staff that moving to competitors would be difficult — according to a former employee.

The company also used non-compete agreements restricting employees from joining rivals for two years, as part of their employment contracts, with senior employees holding valuable technical expertise likely facing closer scrutiny.

But Samsung's concern extended beyond individual departures.

"It wasn't just about one employee leaving. The company wanted to send a message to those who stayed that moving to a competitor wouldn't be easy," the former employee said.

Meanwhile, Chinese semiconductor companies have sought to close the technology gap by recruiting experienced overseas engineers with high salaries, stock options, housing and family support, according to Jeongku Choi, a senior analyst at Counterpoint Research.

Their expertise has helped Chinese chipmakers overcome technical bottlenecks, particularly as U.S. export restrictions have limited China's access to advanced chipmaking equipment, Choi said.

Once viewed as unlikely to catch up with established players, Chinese manufacturers are now seen as "meaningful competitors," she added.

Counterpoint's Memory Tracker for the second quarter of 2026 projected CXMT's share of global DRAM revenue would reach 10%.

Established chipmakers are strengthening legal protections and internal security measures while adjusting compensation packages to retain key engineers, according to Choi.

South Korea has also designated certain semiconductor manufacturing technologies as "National Core Technologies," raising the penalties for unauthorized leaks of those technologies, Choi said.

The legal battle could intensify as Chinese memory companies expand beyond their home market.

"I believe that this management of trade secrets will expand its scope even further to IP and legal areas once Chinese memory companies move beyond the Chinese market and begin to expand their exports," Choi said.

The growing competition is putting greater focus on how semiconductor companies protect the knowledge accumulated by engineers working on complex manufacturing processes.

In Taiwan, home to the world's largest contract chipmaker TSMC, protections for trade secrets have evolved alongside the semiconductor industry’s growing strategic importance.

Jamie J. Yang, a partner specializing in antitrust and intellectual property at Innovatus Law, said Taiwan limits post-employment non-compete agreements to two years and requires employers to compensate former employees at least half their average monthly salary during the restricted period.

Yang added that the complexity of semiconductor manufacturing makes it difficult for any single engineer to possess enough knowledge to replicate an entire production line elsewhere.

"There is just very little chance that one person ... will know enough to replicate that line in any of the other companies," Yang said.

Moves by engineers between rival semiconductor companies could increase modestly as competition for top talent intensifies, while trade secret litigation is also likely to rise as firms seek to protect their technological edge, she added.

In Singapore, Zechariah Chan, a partner at law firm Lee & Lee, said protecting trade secrets can be particularly difficult in the semiconductor industry, where proprietary knowledge often involves intangible manufacturing techniques and methods.

Proving that a competitor has used proprietary manufacturing techniques can also be difficult, as outsiders generally see only the final product, according to Chan.

"There is no bright line between an engineer's expertise and proprietary information," he said. General skills and knowledge stay with the engineer, while trade secrets remain with the company.

In Singapore, non-compete clauses are enforceable only if they protect legitimate business interests and are reasonable in scope, geographical area and duration.

In Hong Kong, courts generally take a restrictive approach to non-compete clauses, enforcing them only when they protect legitimate business interests and go no further than necessary, according to Jezamine Fewins, head of litigation at Lewis Silkin in Hong Kong.

Courts are more willing to protect trade secrets through court injunctions, Fewins said.

"The difficulty is that often the trade secret is carried in the employee's head," she added. Information is more likely to qualify as a trade secret if its disclosure to a competitor could cause significant harm to the former employer and the company has taken steps to restrict access.

For chipmakers, Fewins said the key is to clearly define what constitutes a trade secret, make employees aware of that information and put safeguards in place to limit access.

Samsung and CXMT did not respond to CNBC's request for comment.

#2880. What Steve Kerr told Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg after Luka Dončić trash talk

What Steve Kerr told Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg after Luka Dončić trash talk originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Few rookies draw the spotlight after just one game in the NBA, but Yaxel Lendeborg managed to do so following the Warriors' season-opening loss to the Lakers.

The Warriors rookie's callout of Luka Dončić in his postgame press conference made national headlines. All eyes were on Lendeborg to see how he would follow it up, particularly when the league's scoring leader and the Lakers rolled into Chase Center the following week.

Warriors coach Steve Kerr, speaking Wednesday with ESPN's Ernie Johnson on "Inside the NBA," shared how he coached Lendeborg after the rookie's comments to the media.

"We talked to him as a staff; we talked as a team," Kerr recalled. "Steph [Curry] had a great comment after that Laker game. He said, 'Hey, I love Yaxel's competitive desire, but now he's got to back it up,' and I thought that was perfectly said.

"So, I kind of reiterated that with Yaxel, like, hey — we love this spirit, we love the competitiveness, but just know that Luka's going to be coming."

Dončić, to no surprise, came out of the gates hot in the rematch, scoring 21 points with five 3-pointers made. Golden State was down 38-15 after that first quarter but mounted a furious comeback to finish with a 112-95 win.

Although Lendeborg's fiery comments might have provided some extra motivation for Dončić in his opening flurry, Kerr appreciates the rookie's spirit.

"There's an old saying in coaching — you'd rather rein a guy back in than try to get him going," Kerr said. "And I think that's true with Yaxel. He wants to be great. He's got this competitive spirit.

"So, in terms of reining him in, it's more just — hey, this is what the league is about. You're going to see these kinds of challenges all the time. And do what Steph did: back it up. And Yaxel's been doing that. So, I love coaching him. I think he's got an incredible future."

Lendeborg only scored six points in the rematch with the Lakers but has scored in double digits in each of his other seven games played this season. In his next game after the rematch, Lendeborg scored a career-high 37 points.

Entering Wednesday, Lendeborg ranks third among rookies with 21.5 points per game. He has a chance to improve that Thursday night against the Miami Heat at Chase Center.

The next time Lendeborg will have an opportunity to face Dončić and the Lakers will be Jan. 21.

#2879. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2876. Democrats flip dozens of GOP seats. Republicans haven't flipped a single one

Seven Senate seats. At least 24 House districts. Five governorships. Twelve state legislative chambers. By Wednesday afternoon, that's how far Democrats' historic midterm rout had reached.

Republicans, meanwhile, had yet to flip a single Democratic-held congressional seat, governorship or state legislative chamber, according to Associated Press data. Their remaining opportunities for congressional pickups are largely confined to two newly gerrymandered House districts that weren't even supposed to be competitive in the first place.

The lopsided results underscored the breadth of the GOP's shellacking, with Democrats breaking decades of Republican dominance in reliably red states and reshaping the balance of power from Washington to state capitols.

As of late Wednesday, nine House seats remained uncalled by the AP along with two Senate seats and two governors races. The one seat Republicans may have a chance of flipping that hasn't been redistricted ahead of this election is Maine's 2nd District, FiftyPlusOne head of research Mary Radcliffe said by email.

In Kansas, voters elected their first Democratic U.S. senator since 1932, ending a 94-year losing streak for the party. In Texas, Democrats toppled three-term Republican Gov. Greg Abbott and captured a Senate seat that had eluded the party since 1988. Republican-held Senate seats also fell in Iowa, South Carolina and Ohio, along with seats in Maine and North Carolina.

The same wave swept through governors' mansions. Democrats scored upsets in Georgia, Iowa, Ohio and Nevada — all states President Donald Trump won in 2024 — giving the party a majority of the nation's governorships for the first time since 2010.

Upheaval even reached state capitols, where Republican legislative majorities that had endured for decades fell like dominoes.

Democrats seized control of the Texas House for the first time in more than two decades, flipped both chambers of the Wisconsin Legislature and wrested the North Carolina House from Republicans after 16 years in the minority.

In Pennsylvania, Democrats captured the state Senate for the first time since before 1994, giving the party complete control of state government. They also reclaimed lower chambers in Michigan and Minnesota, restoring full Democratic control in both states. In Arizona, unofficial results pointed toward Democrats taking both legislative chambers for the first time in roughly 60 years.

Even where Republicans held on to legislative majorities, Democrats stripped away veto-proof supermajorities in Florida, Indiana, Kansas and Missouri. Not since the aftermath of Watergate in 1974 had the GOP faced a statehouse reckoning of this magnitude, according to the Democratic Legislative Campaign Committee.

The scale of the losses is all the more remarkable given how aggressively Republicans had tried to protect their power. At Trump's urging, GOP lawmakers spent months pursuing an extraordinary mid-decade redistricting campaign, redrawing congressional boundaries in Texas, Florida and North Carolina to tilt the electoral map in their favor.

Yet the gerrymandering push did little to blunt the Democratic wave. And the damage might not be over. Republicans were still fighting to hold Senate seats in Alaska and Mississippi, with several House contests also yet to be called Wednesday.

— Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#2875. Ex-Lakers standout goes public with much-older new girlfriend Amber Rose on LA date

Christian Wood remains an unsigned free agent after spending the past two seasons with the Lakers.

But that hasn't stopped him from making headlines while in LA.

Photos show the 31-year-old Wood going out on a dinner date with American model and television personality Amber Rose at Matsuhisa, a Beverly Hills restaurant.

Amber Rose and former NBA player Christian Wood leave Matsuhisa restaurant in Beverly Hills, California, Nov. 3, 2026. BACKGRID

Rose is 43 years old, which means there is a 12-year age gap between these two.

Wood has not been in the NBA since the 2023-24 season, when he made 50 appearances with the Lakers and averaged 6.9 points over 17.4 minutes per game. He was waived when Los Angeles signed Alex Len in February 2025.

Rose has dated numerous high-profile celebrities, including Kanye West, Wiz Khalifa (with whom she was married to for over a year and has a son), Machine Gun Kelly, 21 Savage and NBA player Terrence Ross.

Amber Rose speaks during Day 1 of the Republican National Convention at the Fiserv Forum in Milwaukee, Wisconsin, Monday, July 15, 2024. Ron Sachs – CNP for NY Post

Rose also gained a lot of attention for supporting Donald Trump during the 2024 presidential election.

It's hard to imagine that Wood can make his way back into the NBA at this point, given how he has been out of the league for several years.

Christian Wood (35) of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots against the Detroit Pistons on Feb. 13, 2024, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

But perhaps this relationship with Rose can help him remain relevant.
