#2836. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

Not good to see the Eagles' kicker on the report. But guessing the Eagles are managing his workload to get him to game day.

Greenard clearly isn't healthy. The Eagles are managing his reps. Ditto for Hunt.

The Eagles listed eight players under FULL PARTICIPATION: Tanner McKee, Landon Dickerson, Dallas Goedert, Zack Baun, Jordan Davis, Moro Ojomo, Markel Bell, and Kenny Moore II.

All of these players should be in line to play on Sunday.

McKee is a new addition to the report with a chest injury. This development comes at the same time that he's reportedly being benched for Joe Milton III. For some reason, the Eagles are once again being weird about the handling of their QB situation.

Man, imagine if Nick Sirianni didn't focus on competitive advantage so much. The Eagles might be a really bad team without all of this very necessary obfuscation.

Dickerson being listed with a hip injury is new. He was previously dealing with knee and pectoral injuries.

#2832. Leave No Doubt: Pat Terrell, Lou Holtz, and the Play That Helped Change Notre Dame History

As Notre Dame prepares to face Miami once again, a look back at the 1988 classic through the eyes of the man who made one of its most unforgettable plays.

There are certain moments in Notre Dame football history that never seem to get old, no matter how many times we watch them.

Rocket Ismail's electrifying returns. Tony Rice weaving his way through opposing defenses. The final seconds ticking off the clock as Notre Dame celebrated another unforgettable victory.

If you were a Notre Dame fan in 1988, you probably remember exactly where you were when the Fighting Irish defeated the top-ranked Miami Hurricanes, 31–30, in what would forever be remembered as the Catholics vs. Convicts game.

It was October 15, 1988. Notre Dame Stadium was electric, the rivalry was intense, and the Irish were determined to prove they belonged among the nation's elite.

Thirty-eight years later, with Notre Dame preparing to host Miami on Saturday, November 7, I thought it would be fun to revisit that unforgettable afternoon through the eyes of one of the players who helped make it happen.

And thanks to a conversation I had with former Notre Dame defensive back Pat Terrell for my book Echoes From the End Zone – The Men We Became, I have a few stories to share.

Including one about why he didn't catch the football.

One of the things I've always loved about interviewing former Notre Dame players is discovering the stories behind the moments we remember.

We see the touchdowns, the interceptions, the celebrations, and the championships. But we don't always see the struggles, the disappointments, or the unexpected turns that helped shape those players.

Pat's journey was certainly no exception.

Coming to Notre Dame from Florida was an adjustment. He missed home, and the South Bend weather wasn't exactly what he was accustomed to.

But the bigger challenge came on the football field.

Pat arrived at Notre Dame with dreams of becoming a star wide receiver. In fact, he had some pretty lofty expectations for himself.

As he told me, "I just knew as soon as Tim Brown graduated that I would come in and win MY Heisman Trophy."

But football, much like life, doesn't always follow the plan we've carefully mapped out for ourselves.

Instead of becoming Notre Dame's next great wide receiver, Pat was moved to defensive back during his junior year.

For a young man who had envisioned catching touchdown passes and perhaps winning the Heisman Trophy, it was a difficult adjustment.

He suddenly had to learn a new position, compete for playing time, and prove himself all over again.

And he wasn't simply trying to earn a starting job at Notre Dame. He also hoped to play professional football someday.

As Pat explained, he didn't become a starting defensive back until the third game of his junior season.

One of the most memorable defensive plays in Notre Dame football history would come from a young man who had only recently become a starter at his position.

Sometimes the path you never expected turns out to be the one that takes you exactly where you're supposed to go.

To understand the significance of that afternoon, you have to remember what the rivalry between Notre Dame and Miami had become.

The Hurricanes were the defending national champions. They were talented, confident, and accustomed to winning.

Notre Dame, meanwhile, was looking to restore its place among college football's elite under head coach Lou Holtz.

The Irish had endured some painful losses to Miami in previous seasons, including the infamous 58–7 defeat in 1985.

Notre Dame had confidence. It had leadership. And it had a group of players who believed they could compete with anyone in the country.

Pat remembered the tension surrounding the game, including a pregame confrontation between players from the two teams.

He also remembered Coach Holtz's ability to prepare his players, both physically and emotionally, for the biggest moments.

And then there was defensive coordinator Barry Alvarez.

Pat credited Alvarez with helping him become the player he needed to be, particularly given how recently he had moved to the defensive side of the football.

The coaches knew Miami's tendencies. They studied the Hurricanes' offense. And they practiced the situations they expected to encounter.

That preparation would become especially important in the final moments of the game.

Notre Dame led Miami 31–24 late in the fourth quarter when the Hurricanes mounted one final drive.

Miami quarterback Steve Walsh connected with Andre Brown for a touchdown, cutting Notre Dame's lead to 31–30.

Rather than attempt an extra point and settle for a tie, Miami head coach Jimmy Johnson decided to go for two.

It was the kind of decision that can define a rivalry.

And suddenly, Pat Terrell found himself in the middle of one of the most important plays of Notre Dame's season.

Pat told me that the Irish had practiced defending that particular play several times.

He was lined up against Leonard Conley, a Miami player he knew from his high school days in Florida.

Two young men who had competed against one another back home were now facing each other on one of college football's biggest stages.

Pat was prepared for the play, but he was also quick to point out that he didn't make it alone.

George Williams applied pressure to Walsh, affecting the timing of the throw. Pat was in position, read the play, and knocked the pass away.

It was the defensive stop that preserved one of the most celebrated victories in Fighting Irish history.

But when I interviewed Pat, I had a question for him.

Actually, it wasn't entirely my question.

Tony Rice had suggested that I ask Pat why he merely knocked the ball down instead of intercepting it.

After all, wouldn't catching the ball have been even better?

"Tell Tony because I wanted to make it dramatic. ANYONE can catch the ball."

Of course, no discussion of Notre Dame's 1988 national championship season would be complete without talking about Lou Holtz.

Coach Holtz had a remarkable ability to motivate his players, challenge them to believe in themselves, and convince them that they were capable of accomplishing things they might never have imagined.

Pat remembered the famous pregame speech before the Miami game, including a particularly colorful remark directed at Jimmy Johnson.

Let's just say Coach Holtz made it very clear that his players were to take care of business on the football field, and he'd handle the opposing coach.

He described Holtz's speeches as consistently moving and motivational, but this particular one left him especially fired up and ready to play.

Holtz wasn't simply asking his players to compete with Miami.

That belief became part of the identity of the 1988 Fighting Irish.

It also speaks to something I've always admired about Coach Holtz's approach to football and life.

He understood that success wasn't just about talent.

It was about preparation, discipline, confidence, and believing in the people around you.

And perhaps most importantly, it was about leaving no doubt that you had given everything you had.

You might assume that Pat's favorite memory from Notre Dame would be knocking down that two-point conversion against Miami.

After all, it's the play that helped preserve one of the most famous victories in program history.

But when I asked him about his most memorable moment, his answer surprised me.

It was the night after Notre Dame won the national championship.

Pat remembered the joy of celebrating with his teammates and, especially, seeing his parents share in that moment.

He talked about realizing that he and his teammates had accomplished something extraordinary together, something that would connect them for the rest of their lives.

Because when all is said and done, the most meaningful moments in sports aren't always the ones that make the highlight reels.

Sometimes they're the moments spent with the people who helped you get there.

The parents who supported you. The coaches who believed in you. The teammates who pushed you. The friends who celebrated alongside you.

As we prepare for another Notre Dame–Miami matchup this Saturday, I've been thinking about the lessons that players like Pat Terrell carried with them from their time in South Bend.

Pat arrived with one dream, found himself on a completely different path, and ultimately became part of a national championship team.

He learned to adapt, to work hard, to trust his coaches, and to believe that he could make a difference.

That's one of the things I love most about Notre Dame football history.

The stories aren't just about wins and losses. They're about perseverance, leadership, friendship, and the people who help us become the best versions of ourselves.

Those values are also why I wanted to honor Coach Holtz through my Leave No Doubt T-shirts and hoodies.

The design is a tribute to Coach Holtz and the lasting impact he has made on generations of Notre Dame players, students, alumni, and fans.

I'm also proud that a portion of the proceeds from every Leave No Doubt shirt and hoodie sold benefits the Holtz's Heroes Foundation, which carries forward his commitment to making a positive difference in the lives of others.

You can see the shirts, hoodies, and the rest of my Notre Dame-inspired collection here.

As Coach Holtz often reminded us, the goal is to make a positive difference in the lives of others.

And I can't think of a better legacy than that.

So here's to Pat Terrell, the 1988 national champions, Coach Lou Holtz, and all the unforgettable moments that make Notre Dame football history so special.

And to the 2026 Fighting Irish as they prepare to face Miami once again:

Pat Terrell's recollections are drawn from my interview with him for Echoes From the End Zone. The memories and quotations are shared with gratitude for his willingness to tell his story.

#2831. Kyle Shanahan refutes Jay Glazer’s Jauan Jennings report, confirms 49ers FaceTime

One former 49ers receiver is expected back in their building Friday for the first time in almost a year, but Brandon Aiyuk won't be the solution to San Francisco's injury woes at the position.

"There's no absolutes in life," coach Kyle Shanahan said Wednesday, all but shooting down any chance of Aiyuk ever suiting up for the 49ers again, despite his application for reinstatement.

That doesn't mean the 49ers aren't still in search of players who could bolster a receiving corps that may get Mike Evans, Deebo Samuel and Demarcus Robinson back this week but is still undermanned compared to where it was when training camp opened at the start of August.

San Francisco 49ers head coach Kyle Shanahan responds to a question during a news conference in Atlanta, Sunday, Oct. 25, 2026. AP Photo/Brynn Anderson

General manager John Lynch arranged one reunion with the trade for defensive tackle DeForest Buckner, and he isn't the only old friend the 49ers have tried to bring back in the fold.

Shanahan confirmed on Wednesday that the 49ers attempted to swing a deal for Jauan Jennings and that the Vikings were amenable to it but, in the end, Jennings said no, thank you.

"It wasn't, like, a definite no at first," Shanahan said. "But he was like, 'Man, Kyle, I'm pretty happy. I'm liking stuff.' When I heard the way he talked and where we're at, I agreed with him. It was a good conversation and something that was possible, but that's why it didn't go down."

Jennings led the 49ers with 643 receiving yards last season and was second to George Kittle with 975 in 2024, but he left for a contract worth up to $13 million with the Vikings. He reportedly envisioned a larger role that hasn't materialized, with only six catches on 10 targets in six games. But Shanahan clarified that there was no "bad blood" between the two sides.

Minnesota Vikings player Jauan Jennings (14) catches a pass against the Indianapolis Colts in Minneapolis on Sunday, Oct. 25, 2026. Getty Images

Jauan Jennings of the San Francisco 49ers warms up before an NFL playoff game against the Seattle Seahawks in Seattle, Saturday, Jan. 17, 2026. Getty Images

Jay Glazer's initial report on Fox's pregame show Sunday said that Jennings had scuttled the deal and turned down an offer to FaceTime with 49ers brass. Shanahan said that wasn't true.

"We talked for a while," Shanahan said. "He FaceTimed me. … It was a good talk. But he's with good people, he likes where he's at and I thought it made sense what he said."

It's safe to say that of the two former 49ers receivers, Jennings is in better standing than Aiyuk.

"We love Jauan," Shanahan said. "We have such a good relationship with Jauan and know him real well. We didn't want to do it without talking to him first."

San Francisco 49ers general manager John Lynch chats on the field before a game against the Seattle Seahawks in Seattle, Sunday, Oct. 11, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

While that trade didn't work out, Lynch and the front office are still doing their due diligence ahead of Tuesday's 1 p.m. PT trade deadline. San Francisco could still use help at receiver, with rookie De'Zhaun Stribling still "probably over a month away," per Shanahan.

The 49ers are exhausting all their options, not just targeting receivers or former players.

"It's what personnel people do," Shanahan said. "They look every week to see who you can add week in and week out and it gets bigger as the trade deadline comes. But JJ was someone we knew, so it was someone we wanted to talk to if we went through with that. And they were willing to do it at the time. So we wanted to have a conversation with him knowing the guy. Receiver is something we're still looking into, and so is every other position."

#2829. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2828. This 'SNL' alum is a huge Bengals fan and thinks you're doing it wrong

March 6, 2007: Bengals mascot Who Dey clowns around with Oak Hills mascot Highlander at Oak Hills High School.

Aug. 17, 2008: Bengals mascot Who Dey leads the team onto the field before the start of a preseason game with the Detroit Lions at Paul Brown Stadium.

Dec. 11, 2008: Who Dey, the Bengals mascot, leads a group of Highlands Middle School sixth graders in jumping jacks during a visit to the school for the "What Moves U" program. Who Dey distributed Bengals necklaces, miniature footballs and other items to the students.

April 20, 2010: Bengals Mascot Who Dey dances with Debbie Winkler (left) and Carol Bachelier of New Richmond, as they dance to "Cotton-Eyed Joe" with longtime Appalachian Festival dancers Harkie's Hoedowners on Fountain Square.

Dec. 3, 2013: Bengals mascot Who Dey is mobbed by some of the students at the Heritage Elementary School in Liberty Twp.

Bengals mascot Who Dey rings a bell while making an appearance at the Salvation Army Kettle Kick-off on Nov. 19, 2021, in Over-The-Rhine.

Daisy, of Florence, Ky., is held on a leash by the FC Cincinnati, Cincinnati Reds, Cincinnati Cyclones and Cincinnati Bengals mascots during the Greater Cincinnati Polar Plunge 2024.

Bengals mascot Who Dey dances before the preseason game between the Cincinnati Bengals and the Indianapolis Colts at Paycor Stadium on Aug. 22, 2024.

Cincinnati Bengals mascot Who Dey signs autographs during a Christmas shopping event in 2024.

March 6, 2007: Bengals mascot Who Dey clowns around with Oak Hills mascot Highlander at Oak Hills High School.

Aug. 17, 2008: Bengals mascot Who Dey leads the team onto the field before the start of a preseason game with the Detroit Lions at Paul Brown Stadium.

Dec. 11, 2008: Who Dey, the Bengals mascot, leads a group of Highlands Middle School sixth graders in jumping jacks during a visit to the school for the "What Moves U" program. Who Dey distributed Bengals necklaces, miniature footballs and other items to the students.

April 20, 2010: Bengals Mascot Who Dey dances with Debbie Winkler (left) and Carol Bachelier of New Richmond, as they dance to "Cotton-Eyed Joe" with longtime Appalachian Festival dancers Harkie's Hoedowners on Fountain Square.

Dec. 3, 2013: Bengals mascot Who Dey is mobbed by some of the students at the Heritage Elementary School in Liberty Twp.

Bengals mascot Who Dey rings a bell while making an appearance at the Salvation Army Kettle Kick-off on Nov. 19, 2021, in Over-The-Rhine.

Daisy, of Florence, Ky., is held on a leash by the FC Cincinnati, Cincinnati Reds, Cincinnati Cyclones and Cincinnati Bengals mascots during the Greater Cincinnati Polar Plunge 2024.

Bengals mascot Who Dey dances before the preseason game between the Cincinnati Bengals and the Indianapolis Colts at Paycor Stadium on Aug. 22, 2024.

Cincinnati Bengals mascot Who Dey signs autographs during a Christmas shopping event in 2024.

Greater Cincinnati is filled with passionate football fans (even if they don't root for the Bengals), but one Cincinnati native takes football season very seriously.

True to her hometown, born-and-bred Cincinnatian Jasmine Pierce is a self-proclaimed "sports gal" and ride-or-die for the Bengals, especially the team's three core players: Joe Burrow, Tee Higgins and Ja'Marr Chase.

Pierce, the Los Angeles and (sometimes) New York-based writer and producer, who's worked on shows including "Saturday Night Live," "Big Mouth" and "The Tonight Show starring Jimmy Fallon," attributes much of her success on the school level to Cincinnati's School for Creative and Performing Arts, where she attended high school.

After that, she wanted to branch out and ended up at New York University. But no matter where she goes, her love for the Bengals and the Queen City remains. So much so that the last time she was in the Big Apple, she took the moment to talk about the Bengals on the highly popular internet show SubwayTakes.

Pierce joined SubwayTakes, the New York-based internet talk show hosted by comedian Kareem Rahma, on June 26, decked out in a custom Tee Higgins jersey-turned-corset (which she made).

During her appearance, she expressed her love for the Bengals and dished out some NFL hot takes, like how playing fantasy football makes you not truly loyal to your team.

"If even part of you is rooting for your team to win, but not quite too much, or for your team to do well, but for one player to not do well, or if you're rooting for your team to win but you care about the spread, then that's not loyalty, and it's disgusting to me."

When asked if she had a favorite between Chase and Higgins, Pierce stood her ground that she loves both of them but wants to push them to be better. (She also noted that Chad Johnson, aka Ochocinco, was one of her biggest crushes in high school.)

"Listen, I love Ja'Marr, so there's nothing about thinking one is necessarily better. I think they're equally good ... and I think that they are good motivating forces for each other."

And when it comes to her favorite player, it should come as no surprise that it's Joe Burrow, and it's not because of his looks (even if Pierce does think "he's hot").

"I love his mentality. I love the way he plays. I love the calmness. I love his attention to detail. I love his purposefulness. I love his focus and his drive. Like, there are so many things about him that I'm just like, if I could have built a perfect quarterback, I mean, I would have probably made him Black. But aside from that, everything else about him I think is just so ideal," she said.

While the internet show looks like it happens on the fly in New York's subway system. Pierce, who often returns to the Big Apple, decided to do the show while in town for the 2026 Roots Picnic.

Naturally, Pierce seized the moment to don her Bengals gear and make the segment mostly about her hometown team (and if you follow her on social media, you know she's passionate about them).

"I definitely packed a few options. I always probably have at least one Bengals thing with me just because it's so much of my wardrobe," she said, adding that she wasn't sure what outfit she'd wear but sporting Bengals "felt like the right thing to do."

Pierce, whose love for sports grew from being raised in the same home as her mother and all her grandparents, said she's spent the last few years learning how to sew specifically so she could upcycle her old Bengals jerseys.

She has about 30 Bengals jerseys now. "I have all these jerseys like Rudy Johnson and T.J. Houshmandzadeh and all these other players who I love, and I still want to celebrate," she said.

Women cementing their place in the NFL world, especially with fashion, is something Pierce thinks the league is "intentionally doing," since there's a bigger market for women in football and sports in general. This has been especially true thanks to Taylor Swift's emergence in the NFL world. According to research published by Quantumrun, the female audience has grown to 47% of the total U.S. fanbase.

"There are a lot more groups popping up, social media accounts popping up about women who are specifically obsessed with football. And that's been such a relief to me as a woman who often goes to sports bars and hears like, 'Oh, wow, you really know your stuff.' Like, yeah, I do!"

She also attributed her "abnormal obsession with football" to growing up in Cincinnati.

"Certainly other cities have football obsessions, but Cincinnati fans' loyalty, specifically, is unmatched. That's pretty much what my SubwayTake was about. I feel very strongly that we should be loyal and passionate toward our teams. But it was weird coming out and realizing that my fandom was so much more insane than other people's."

Pierce doesn't make the rules, but if it were up to her, there would be no negative talk when it came to the Cincinnati Bengals, especially from Queen City natives. Now, this doesn't mean the team can't be criticized or critiqued, but talking down on the team and players is not something a true fan would do, according to her.

"Suffer with them. Be there. We're there to support our team," she said. "Support involves being there when things are hard or things aren't going well. And then, like, have criticism, certainly. But it can be thoughtful, useful criticism."

Pierce attributes her crazy fanaticism to her grandfather, or "Papa," who was "super into the Reds" growing up. They rooted for the Bengals, but they couldn't always afford football tickets, so attending Reds games was the next best thing.

"He was the one that made me so strict and loyal. He's very adamant that the game's not over till it's over. We were never allowed to leave. And I think that is a pretty strong sentiment in Cincinnati in general," she said. "There are certainly some people, but you don't see the mass abandonment at the end of games in the same way that I see at other stadiums."

She might not reside in Cincinnati and hasn't in quite some time, but the city and its teams are always on her mind. It's a testament not only to the Bengals' fan base, but the city itself.

"I don't know any Bengals fans that stopped being Bengals fans," she said. "We might be mad. We might watch fewer games, but I don't know any Bengals fans that switch to other teams. I don't know any that just completely dissolved their loyalty.

"We're just mad and pouting in the corner until something gets better, or we're always there. And I think that's my favorite thing about Cincinnati and why I'm so loyal, because that loyalty is given back."

Now that Pierce lives in Los Angeles, she often frequents the city's Bengals bar, but it's not the same as attending a game in person. The 36-year-old has made it her life goal to see the Bengals play every NFL team in their own home stadium.

#2826. Maxx Crosby: I love being a Raider, and this is where I want to be

The Raiders have traded Maxx Crosby once, but the Ravens reneged on the deal.

A report this week indicated that the Raiders have "no plans" to trade the edge rusher again, and he doesn't want them to.

"I love being a Raider. I've said that a million times," Crosby said Wednesday, via video from Raider Nation Radio. "This is where I want to be. Super excited, obviously for the present, but also for what's to come."

The Ravens sent two first-round picks to the Raiders for Crosby. The Ravens, though, failed him on his physical and sent him back to Las Vegas, because of long-term concerns about Crosby's surgically repaired left knee.

Crosby, 29, likely could still bring two first-round picks to the Raiders. He has 22 tackles, three sacks, a forced fumble, a pass defensed and 18 quarterback hits this season.

Crosby has played 88 percent of the team's defensive snaps.

He expressed optimism at where the Raiders are headed short term and long term.

"I'm not thinking too far down the road. I'm being present," Crosby said. "We're 4-4. We've got a really good football team. I love this locker room. I love the guys. We have great leadership. Klint [Kubiak] is a really good coach. We have Robby [Leonard], who is a first time being a DC. He's doing a hell of a job. We've got a lot of new faces, new pieces, a lot of young guys as well.

"I love where we're going. We've got a lot of work to do. We know that. That's the beauty in it. Everyone can say whatever they want. We had this conversation last week [after a 35-21 loss to the Rams], and it was like the world is ending, and now it's all these positive vibes. For us, it's about focus about this week, today, getting better. That's the mindset of this whole team. I'm excited about everything."

#2824. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2823. MLB Network’s Intentional Talk Reversal Proves Connection Is Media’s Most Valuable Currency

In media, we talk a lot about the currency of connection. It's the ability of a brand or talent to find ways to attach itself to the everyday consumer and become an important part of that person's daily life. It's difficult to earn and even harder to maintain in an increasingly crowded content space that continues to grow. When Intentional Talk announced last month that the show would be ending its 16-year run on MLB Network, baseball fans and players alike demonstrated just how strong their connection to the program had become.

Social media was buzzing. Commentators across traditional and digital media were aghast. How could MLB Network decide to cut its flagship program when so much uncertainty lies ahead this winter for the sport itself? The reaction was loud, and MLB heard it. On Wednesday, MLB reversed course and chose connection over cost.

It goes without saying that Major League Baseball has a lot on its plate at the moment. The glory and spotlight of the World Series are now in the rearview mirror. In addition, the hangovers from the Milwaukee Brewers' first World Series parade have begun to fade. Ahead lies the expiration of the collective bargaining agreement and the potential for a long, drawn-out negotiation.

Yet, amid all the noise and the storm ahead, MLB wanted to ensure that one element of the baseball experience wouldn't disappear. The one hour each weekday that belonged to Kevin Millar, Ryan Dempster and Siera Santos.

Yesterday was originally scheduled to be Intentional Talk's finale. Moreover, the MLB Network website's television schedule even listed the program's timeslot as "Final Show."

Instead, the episode became a celebration that was never planned. The show wrapped up its 16th season on MLB Network with a "see you next season." Instead of the depressing "see you down the road." While the reasons behind the decision to end Intentional Talk were never revealed, the reasons to keep it were evident.

Connection is currency. In media, it's among the most valuable assets any brand or talent can own. Intentional Talk was never a ratings juggernaut, crushing viewership records year after year. The program rarely broke major news or leaned into the kind of hot takes that drive engagement online.

Instead, Intentional Talk earned its connection through honest, revealing and entertaining conversations that celebrated the game and the personalities surrounding it. For years, I heard people talk about how baseball doesn't do enough to market its biggest personalities. Of course, superstars from large-market teams are often the ones featured in national advertising campaigns.

However, the underlying roster of talent and the personalities behind those players represent a largely untapped source of interest in the sport. That's where Intentional Talk swung for the fence.

Intentional Talk was the vehicle that connected fans to that layer of baseball through personality. It took a game some consider boring and made it entertaining by showcasing its lighter side. More importantly, it gave players an opportunity to reveal their personalities. They could crack jokes, answer trivia questions and connect with audiences in ways that no home run or postgame press conference could replicate.

Intentional Talk has become, and continues to be, your favorite baseball player's favorite baseball show.

It shouldn't be surprising that the outpouring of support for the program ultimately tipped the scales and prompted MLB to reconsider its decision. I wish more companies would put the viewer's perspective first when making programming decisions rather than allowing other considerations to take precedence.

Yes, shows and talent come and go. That's the nature of the industry. However, some programs are outliers that become incredibly difficult to replace. We have this discussion all the time about Pardon the Interruption, Inside the NBA and several other programs. They are built around identities that connect with viewers and make them feel invested in the entertainment value of sports.

Intentional Talk should serve as a reminder to every show, network and talent in sports media that the most valuable connection you can build doesn't necessarily come through the biggest interview, the loudest opinion or the most viral moment. It comes from finding a way to make sports more personal for the people watching.

Millar, Dempster and Santos, along with former hosts Chris Rose and Stephen Nelson, found that pathway through baseball. They created a space where fans could connect with the personalities behind the game rather than simply the results on the field.

Every show and every talent should be looking for its own version of that connection. What makes your audience feel invested? What makes people want to spend an hour with you when they have countless other options? And what can you offer that makes your relationship with them difficult to replace?

In an industry where programming decisions are often dictated by ratings, viewership and the bottom line. Intentional Talk demonstrated that audience connection still carries tremendous value over all that. Moreover, you can't always control the decisions made above you, the direction of the industry or how long you have a platform. But you can control the relationships you build with your audience.

If there's one lesson to take from Intentional Talk's 16-year run and reverse course by MLB Network, it's that finding your own pathway to connection through sports might be the best investment you can make in securing your future in sports media.

Barrett Media produces daily content on the music, news, and sports media industries. Sign up for our newsletters to stay updated and get the latest information right in your inbox.

#2821. Luka Doncic’s underrated superpower making giant difference for Lakers

Luka Dončić's scoring is dominating headlines.

But a huge reason the Lakers are soaring right now is his passing.

He had 13 assists in Monday's 138-117 win over the Heat as the team finished with 39 assists, the most they've had in any game since Lakers coach JJ Redick took over the helm in June 2024.

When the ball is moving like that, the Lakers are very difficult to defend.

Doncic's passing is an underrated part of his game, overshadowed by his glitzier scoring deluges that drop jaws. But his passing is just as important. It's what makes his teams dangerous.

He reads defenses in a fraction of a second as though he were doing advanced calculus through throngs of hands trying to obscure his vision.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic, right, is defended by Miami Heat forward Andrew Wiggins during an NBA game Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

In many ways, that's his real super power.

"He can make plays, every play in the book that you need to make as a point-of-attack guy," Heat coach Erik Spoelstra said after the Lakers' 138-117 win on Monday.

"You have to pick him up 30 feet. He can make every pass in the book. He can read your defense. He understands what we're trying to do, positions guys in the right spots and he makes the right play over and over and over. That's what the greats do."

With Doncic regularly drawing double- and triple-teams, Redick has made decisiveness a major point of emphasis for the Lakers this season.

"It's the thing we talked about before the game: just shoot, drive, kick it," Redick said. "Just a lot of 0.5-[second] decision making."

The Lakers executed against the Heat, making a defense laden with superstars such as Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo look discombobulated and hapless as they scrambled to slow down Doncic while also stopping leaks.

Sure, Doncic scored an eye-popping 42 points, his fourth 40-point performance in his last five games.

But what really made the Lakers unstoppable?

As a team, they shot 57.3% from the field and 44.2% from beyond the arc. From top to bottom, everyone was starring in their roles.

The Heat's blitzes on Doncic led to him dishing the ball out to Sandro Mamukelashvili and Quentin Grimes for wide open 3s.

Walker Kessler also capitalized on those moments, finishing with his third straight double-double in a 15-point and 15-rebound performance against the Heat.

Antetokounmpo called Doncic "one of the best scorers to ever play this game," but added what Doncic is doing behind the scenes is truly what's making the Lakers elite.

"You can see the message, after every double team he was telling them, like, 'Hey, be aggressive, get downhill,'" Antetokounmpo said. "That fills players up with confidence. You see Sando was aggressive, Kessler was aggressive, Thybulle was aggressive, Grimes — all of them."

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers dribbles during a game against the Miami Heat on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

Doncic is leading the league in scoring with 38.3 points per game, but he's also third in assists (8.1).

When Doncic is taking advantage of the attention he draws and his teammates are starring in their roles, the Lakers are hard to beat. They currently have the best offensive rating in the league (121.9).

For a Lakers team with nine new players, including Kessler, it has been a learning process. Let's not forget the Lakers blowing a 25-point lead against the Warriors on Oct. 30 when Golden State dialed up its defensive pressure.

Playing with a guy like Doncic requires unique skills.

Luka Doncic #77 of the Los Angeles Lakers plays defense on Giannis Antetokounmpo #7 of the Miami Heat on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Crypto.Com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

His teammates must always be ready. They have to react instantaneously. They have to be fearless.

And if his teammates make the right reactions, defenses are stunted.

"I think at the beginning of the season, we didn't execute well on double teams," Doncic said. "But I just thought [Monday], I think we improved a lot. Everybody was in the right spots. So it's not just me, I think it's everybody, just being in the right spot, being aggressive and shooting with confidence."

Eight games into the season, it appears guys are adjusting well to Luka Magic.

That makes the Lakers one of the most dangerous teams in the league.

#2817. What's next for Rams after season-ending injury to Jaylen Watson?

Just when it looked like the Los Angeles Rams were returning to full strength, the injury bug bites again.

Cornerback Jaylen Watson needs shoulder surgery and will miss the rest of the season, head coach Sean McVay told reporters on Wednesday. Watson exited Sunday's Week 8 win over the Los Angeles Chargers early after re-aggravating the same shoulder originally dislocated in Week 3. He missed the following week but has played every game since, and McVay said on Monday they'd be evaluating the fifth-year DB on a day-to-day basis.

McVay said Wednesday that Watson had hoped to continue to play through the injury with a brace, but the team ultimately decided to shut him down after they were told by doctors "the likelihood of it coming out and doing further damage to it is a very high probability."

"He's been awesome," McVay said. "He's been playing through some different stuff, but he's been really productive. And it's unfortunate that that happened, but I know he wants to be able to play, but it's the right thing to do for him. And that's the tough part about this."

Watson signed a three-year, $51 million contract with the Rams in March shortly after the team traded his former Kansas City Chiefs teammate Trent McDuffie in a blockbuster trade and signing him to a four-year, $124 million extension. The secondary tandem won two Super Bowls as members of the Chiefs and their continued pairing in LA, along with the later acquisition of Myles Garrett, immediately pushed the Rams into offseason favorites for Super Bowl 61, which will be played at SoFi Stadium.

All three have dealt with injuries that have limited their time together on the field. Garrett underwent arthroscopic surgery on his left knee after Week 1 and has been on injured reserve, though he returned to practice this week as a limited participant. McDuffie returned in Week 8 after missing the previous two weeks with a groin injury.

Watson has been a bright spot on the Rams defense, even with playing through pain. The Washington State product has an interception, nine passes defended and 24 tackles in seven games played this season. He has the seventh-highest grade among NFL cornerbacks (80.7), according to Pro Football Focus.

"He's been everything and that much more that we had hoped," McVay said.

#2815. Warriors owner admits Yaxel Lendeborg has changed his thoughts on making trades

Warriors owner admits Yaxel Lendeborg has changed his thoughts on making trades originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

It's been a very impressive start to the season for Golden State Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg. There was a lot of hope that Lendeborg was going to be this type of player when the Warriors drafted him in the first round, though there were also some concerns about him not being this because of his age.

Ultimately, the Warriors didn't worry too much about his age and decided to go get the player who they thought was the best available at the time.

Right now, there's a strong argument that Lendeborg is the best rookie in the NBA. It's also fair to mention that the Warriors' window could be extended because of how he looks.

Stephen Curry is only getting older, and that's a problem for the Warriors trying to compete for the next decade, as he's 38 years old and only has a few seasons left, but it does help the team make some decisions on trying to compete right now.

With Curry playing at this level, averaging a ridiculous 30 points per game at his age, including shooting 42.9% from 3-point range, and Lendeborg averaging 21.5 points and proving that he's a serviceable player on both sides of the floor, it could cause the Warriors to go out and make the necessary moves to improve over the next couple of months.

According to Golden State's owner, it changes the outlook of how the team is going to make decisions over the next couple of months.

"We got this one right. I was 100% sold that this was the guy we wanted in the draft. This guy is the real deal. Yeah, it changes our outlook about what we might wanna do," owner Joe Lacob said, H/T Heavy.

The Warriors are in a position to make a move or two, but it won't be easy because of limited assets.

The Warriors do have a couple of players they could deal, but it doesn't always feel like they're in a position to make a blockbuster deal with the assets they have. Lendeborg and Curry together, if they can continue playing at this level and bring in a third guy, could be all the team needs, however.

#2812. Seahawks sign former UFL MVP quarterback following Sam Darnold injury

The Seattle Seahawks have a banged up quarterback room, and unlike the last time Sam Darnold was injured, they are adding another player to the roster.

Prior to the start of Wednesday's practice, the Seahawks signed former Nebraska and Kansas State quarterback Adrian Martinez, who was most recently with the San Francisco 49ers. This comes after Darnold was ruled out of Week 9 against the Arizona Cardinals due to a quad injury, in addition to a cracked rib suffered in Monday's loss to the Chicago Bears. Drew Lock is slated to start, but he's dealing with a right elbow injury that caused him to miss the last two games, and therefore Martinez joins the practice squad as some insurance.

Martinez's career highlight at the professional level was not in the NFL, but the UFL. As starting quarterback of the Birmingham Stallions, Martinez compiled a 6-1 record as a starter, throwing 15 touchdown passes and only 3 interceptions, while also rushing for over 500 yards and 3 more TDs. His performances earned him league MVP honors. In the 2024 UFL Championship against the San Antonio Brahmas, Martinez was named game MVP after throwing a touchdown to former Seahawks draft pick Gary Jennings Jr, while adding a couple of scores on the ground in a 25-0 victory.

If Lock is still limited due to his elbow injury, it's more likely than not that Martinez will be a game day elevation against the Cardinals this Sunday.

Seattle also brought Jamie Sheriff back to the practice squad after his brief stint with the Los Angeles Rams. Wide receiver Malick Meiga and linebacker Jimmy Ciarlo were released to make room for Sheriff and Martinez.

#2810. Yankees’ Aaron Judge unfairly called out because of Jazz Chisholm Jr’s reported attitude

Yankees' Aaron Judge unfairly called out because of Jazz Chisholm Jr's reported attitude originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

There seems to be an expectation around Major League Baseball that the New York Yankees aren't going to bring Jazz Chisholm Jr. back.

Not extending him a qualifying offer seems all but guaranteed right now, though anything can happen in free agency.

If Chisholm doesn't find the market he's looking for and the Yankees can get him at a relatively low price, bringing him back on a short-term deal wouldn't be the worst idea.

As the Yankees get prepared for some other moves they have to make over the next couple of months, it is at least something they'll have to consider. However, the problem isn't with his play, as many have reported multiple times now that his attitude hasn't made many in the Yankees' front office or clubhouse too happy.

The latest suggestion, however, is a bit unfair to slugger Aaron Judge. Judge is undoubtedly one of the best players in Major League Baseball and the Yankees' captain.

Unfortunately, he dealt with some injuries this season, but he was still the guy the Yankees relied on to be the voice in the locker room. According to one NJ.com writer, however, he blames Judge because of Chisholm's attitude and antics.

"I'll start with this: All the stuff that Jazz Chisholm got away with the last few years and that clubhouse culture stuff you're hearing from CC Sabathia, Joe Girardi, Alex Rodriguez, Jorge Posada and other Yankees alums who won World Series is partially an indictment on Aaron Judge. He is the captain. I know Judge is a great leader because I've heard it from almost every player who's been in the Yankees clubhouse going back to 2017, Judge's rookie year.

"You can't find a better teammate. He calls team meetings to give pep talks and point out mistakes. But I think the Yankees had problems this year that weren't stopped — Jazz's antics, players getting picked off base, fundamental mistakes. I think that needs to change and I'd like to see Judge be a little tougher. I think if he was tougher, some of this stuff would stop because Judge is so respected," Randy Miller of NJ.com wrote.

As a captain, Judge has a job to ensure guys are doing and saying the right things. However, it's also fair to mention that Chisholm is a grown man who makes his own decisions.

Judge can't control him going out to second base with a lollipop in his mouth, making comments to the media, and showing some other frustrating parts.

It's easy to point fingers, but the reality is that the Yankees' captain can only do so much when it comes to problems like this.

#2809. Dianna Russini gets out the word that she's coming back to work next week

The halfway point of the 2026 NFL season arrives this weekend. Next week, Dianna Russini apparently will return to sports media.

Per a source with knowledge of the situation, Russini has begun getting the word out to people in the league that she’ll be returning to work “next week.”

It’s unclear where she’ll be working, or what she’ll be doing.

She resigned from The Athletic in April, one week after photos were published by the New York Post of Russini and Patriots coach Mike Vrabel at a resort in Arizona. In both her comments to the Post and her resignation letter to The Athletic, she denied that there was any type of impropriety.

The Athletic conducted a review of her work. Its findings were released in early September.

“Russini’s relationship with Vrabel was in clear violation of The Athletic’s standards,” wrote Mike Semel of The Athletic, who conducted the investigation. “Regardless of whether the relationship was romantic, it was a breach and should have been disclosed.”

Beyond the relationship was the manner in which she handled the emergence of the controversy.

"[W]hen first confronted with the photos by The New York Post,” Semel wrote, “Russini waited two days before telling her editors and managers. She then told a story to explain the photos that did not align with facts, raising issues of trust.”

Along the way, a strange story emerged regarding Russini being pulled over for texting while driving — made stranger by her decision to tell the story publicly and embellish the notion that she FaceTimed a head coach for help in getting out of the ticket.

So if she’ll be reporting again, she’ll need to restore her own credibility. Especially given the unique set of facts that resulted in her departure from The Athletic.

#2808. Myles Garrett returns to Rams practice; return to play unclear

Rams star edge rusher Myles Garrett, who has been sidelined for seven games following knee surgery, returned to practice Wednesday.

Garrett, the reigning NFL defensive player of the year, has been on injured reserve since undergoing left knee surgery after the Rams' season-opening loss to the San Francisco 49ers in Australia.

Coach Sean McVay said the Rams have attempted to be "as diligent, as thorough" as possible before allowing Garrett to practice and opening the 21-day window for him to return to the roster.

McVay said Garrett would take half the amount of full reps on Wednesday and would be evaluated each day before determining when he would be activated to the roster for a game. Garrett went through individual drills with edge rushers during the portion of practice open to reporters.

The Rams play the Washington Commanders on Sunday in Landover, Md.

"A lot of it is going to be predicated on his communication and feedback to us," McVay said of when Garrett will play in a game for the first time, "and then ultimately being able to see that, hopefully the expectation is he's ready to rock this week."

Read more: Rams cornerback Jaylen Watson to miss rest of season after shoulder surgery

When Garrett returns to game action, it will mark the first time that he and defensive lineman Aaron Donald will play together.

The two stars have five NFL defensive-player-of-the-year awards and 236½ sacks between them.

Donald, sparked by the Rams' trade for Garrett in June, came out of a two-year retirement to rejoin a team that opened the season as the Super Bowl favorite. But Donald, a three-time defensive player of the year, did so on the eve of the opener and did not travel for the first game.

Garrett did not make a tackle against the 49ers. In six games, Donald has 12 tackles, three for losses, and two quarterback hits but no sacks.

Last season, Garrett amassed an NFL season-record 23 sacks for the Cleveland Browns.

#2807. Klay Thompson heating up just in time to bury Warriors in Golden State return

SAN FRANCISCO — The same Klay Thompson will be back in the building where he helped build the Warriors' dynasty Thursday night, but he won't look quite the same.

The second Splash Brother is on his second team since going solo.

Yes, it's still an odd experience for Steve Kerr seeing Thompson rise and fire in a No. 11 uniform that isn't blue and gold. But after four previous trips back to the Chase Center and seven matchups total since their breakup, it doesn't quite shock the eyes as much as it once did.

"It'll always be a little strange seeing [him] in a different uniform. But it's getting a little less strange," Kerr said. "Those first couple times were really bizarre. And then you get used to it.

"It's a reminder that most of us in the NBA end up on multiple teams."

Miami Heat forward Klay Thompson (11) celebrates after scoring against the Indiana Pacers in an NBA Cup basketball game in Miami, Friday, Oct. 30, 2026. AP Photo/Lynne Sladky

Thompson spent 13 years in the Bay Area, won four championships, rehabbed from two catastrophic leg injuries and drained more than 2,500 3s. Born and raised in Los Angeles, Thompson fell in love with the region — and its fans embraced him just as tightly.

As Kerr alluded to, few things last forever in the NBA. Thompson's run with Golden State reached its end when he wanted more money and a bigger role than the Warriors were willing to offer, coming off his age 33 season and on the wrong side of the actuarial curve.

Before the season, Stephen Curry said, "I miss that dude everyday."

Golden State Warriors head coach Steve Kerr, left, talks with Stephen Curry during a game against the Phoenix Suns in Phoenix, Sunday, Oct. 25, 2026. AP Photo/Ross D. Franklin

But that doesn't mean Thompson is any less inclined to take it to his former team Thursday night. Seeing Kerr and Curry and Draymond Green only makes him want it more.

"I want to beat them bad," Thompson told reporters in Miami. "We're all still very competitive in our mid-30s and I love to go compete against them because they're some of my best friends."

It might be a challenge for a Miami team that was handed its fifth loss in seven games to begin this season Monday night in Los Angeles, a more traditional homecoming for Thompson, who grew up watching his dad, Mychal, wear a Lakers uniform for the final five years of his playing career.

Klay Thompson of the Miami Heat arrives before a game against the Los Angeles Lakers in Los Angeles on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Thompson poured in 19 points in the loss and seems to be heating up just at the right time to put on a show against his former team. After knocking down only 12 of his first 37 3-point tries to start the season (32.4%), he's sunk 11 on 19 attempts over his past two games.

After being reduced to a bench role for most of his second season with the Mavericks, Thompson has started all of Miami's first seven games, averaging 11.9 points in 21.9 minutes.

His name won't be the only one to receive loud cheer during introductions, and no, Giannis Antetokounmpo, isn't expecting a warm welcome after spurning the Warriors' advances.

Klay Thompson of the Miami Heat celebrates during a game against the Indiana Pacers at Kaseya Center in Miami on Friday, Oct. 30, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Miami Heat guard Klay Thompson (11) shoots a 3-point basket against Indiana Pacers guard Quenton Jackson (29) on Friday, Oct. 30, 2026, in Miami. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Two-fifths of the Heat's starting five is composed of players who started for the Warriors when they beat the Celtics in Game 6 of the 2022 NBA Finals to clinch their fourth title.

By signing with Miami, Thompson teamed up again with Andrew Wiggins, who was traded there in the deal that brought Jimmy Butler to Golden State. Unlike Thompson, Wiggins has only made one previous trip back to Chase Center since the trade.

It's taken some time for Wiggins to fit in around Antetokounmpo, averaging a career-worst 10.3 points per game while shooting 29.4% from 3, almost seven points below his career rate.

He hasn't scored more than 18 points in any of his previous three rematches with the Warriors. Thompson went off for 22 and 29 in his first two games back against Golden State, but in five matchups since, he's been held to single digits twice, averaging 11.2 per game.

Andrew Wiggins (22) of the Miami Heat drives to the basket against the Indiana Pacers on Friday, Oct. 30, 2026, at Kaseya Center in Miami. NBAE via Getty Images

"This is a big part of the business. It's a small circle, a small community. You get to know each other. You end up competing against friends – sometimes even family," Kerr said. "It'll be great to see both guys — Klay and Wiggs. Two just incredible contributors to the Warriors championships, to the culture, just the vibe of the team. Both Klay and Wiggs are wonderful human beings and great teammates. It'll be good to see them."

The Heat have two free days after Thursday's game before they head up the I-80 corridor to Sacramento to face the Kings on Sunday. They plan to stay in San Francisco, giving the former Warriors teammates plenty of time to wax nostalgic if they so choose.

Maybe Thompson will even take up the opportunity to reprise his daily commute, navigating the water on the San Francisco Bay from his Tiburon home to the Warriors' waterfront facility.

"I just can't wait to go back and see familiar faces," he said. "Not just the players, but the training staff, the equipment managers, the people who work the stands and the concessions, the custodians. I have friends for life in the Bay and LA, so it's always special."

#2806. Ryan Nugent-Hopkins gives emotional reaction to Oilers tribute amid major milestone

Ryan Nugent-Hopkins gives emotional reaction to Oilers tribute amid major milestone appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Only two players in the history of the Edmonton Oilers have played 1000 or more games for the franchise. Defenseman Kevin Lowe played 1037 games in an Oilers sweater during his career. On Tuesday, Ryan Nugent-Hopkins surpassed him for the most games played in franchise history.

Nugent-Hopkins was the first overall pick of the 2011 NHL Draft. "Nuge" didn't exactly become the superstar sort of talent many expected back then. But his career has been nothing short of remarkable. After Tuesday's game, the Oilers star reacted to an in-game tribute marking his milestone.

"I feel the love," Nugent-Hopkins said, via NHL.com. "Having a video that they shared with me was definitely special. To have experienced a lot of these games with a few of the guys in here, it's pretty special. I'm very lucky and fortunate to have been able to hit this milestone with this team."

Unfortunately, Edmonton was unable to get Nugent-Hopkins a win to celebrate his big milestone. The Oilers lost to Connor Hellebuyck and the New Jersey Devils in the shootout. Edmonton got a point in the standings, but couldn't finish out the game.

Nugent-Hopkins made an impact in this contest. He scored a goal and added an assist to aid in the effort. This season, he has scored four goals and nine points in seven games.

The veteran forward has had one incredible career to this point. He still has a few seasons left in him, as well. Let's see how he continues contributing to the Oilers on the ice as the 2026-27 campaign rolls along.

#2803. Five Plays: Stanford First Quarter

Louisville started the game with an unbelievable script, attacking the edges of Stanford's defense. They ran some option plays and a nice swing pass to Isaac Brown early on, looking to get their fast players into space against a Stanford defense that is not very athletic.

I really like that they also looked to take advantage of Stanford's aggressive man coverage on the outside. It was obvious that the plan was to get Tre Richardson the ball in this game, and this go route down the sideline was a great way to start. Lincoln Kienholz throws a nice ball here, but I would have loved to see him put this ball on more of a rope to lead Richardson down the field.

I'm fairly certain that this play is designed to go to Brody Foley across the middle of the field on a delayed crossing route. It looks to me that Lincoln Kienholz just doesn't make the throw even though it's open. They only need 3 yards on this play, so Kienholz should put this on Foley to pick up the short gain.

I think Kienholz sees the linebacker as he works to the left, but doesn't realize he is far enough away from Foley that he won't be able to make a play on the ball. I've pointed it out a lot this season, but he has to let his playmakers go to work for him. Foley is 6-6/260. I like his chances to get a few yards here.

Louisville continues to struggle in zone coverage as we see on this play. Louisville's linebackers were not asked to get downhill in run support in this game. It's a frustrating aspect of the defensive game plan, but it is how the defense is called. With them not being asked to support the run, it is more frustrating that they bite on this very basic play action.

The linebackers end up frozen in the middle of the field while Stanford's receivers run right past them into a giant void in the defense. This play is designed for this outcome, which should be acknowledged, but my issue is that the linebackers on this play are not in a position to make a play on any play that could be called. This is just too easy for the offense to get a first down.

This play worked out for the defense to get a third down stop, but Stanford converted on the next play. I was actually surprised Davis Warren didn't wait for a receiver further down the field to get open so he could get the first down. He had time to wait because he easily identified that Louisville would not be blitzing on this play, even though they had the linebackers showing blitz.

This play was easy to read for Warren because the Cards put themselves into an obvious numbers disadvantage if they actually blitzed on this play. There are two defenders to cover three receivers to the bottom of the screen, and when the Stanford receiver goes in motion, no one on the defense moves. It's a little thing, but this is the type of nuanced stuff that the defense should have nailed down by this point in the season.

I watched this play more times than I can remember, and I could not tell you what the defensive call was on this play. My only guess is that Kaleb Beasley is supposed to count the running back as a passing option here, which would mean that he should not blitz on this play.

So, the call would be that if the running back was to the left of the quarterback, Beasley should blitz, as there is only one "threat" to the top of the screen. However, if this were a slot receiver, he would drop into a zone because there are two receivers to that side. The back doesn't stay in to block, so there are two threats for the linebacker to account for. Warren completes an easy pass to the guy who is left uncovered. The zone coverage continues to be an issue.

#2796. Buccaneers linebacker Alex Anzalone named NFC Defensive Player of the Week

Tampa Bay Buccaneers linebacker Alex Anzalone has been named the NFC Defensive Player of the Week following his standout performance in the team's 16-0 shutout victory over the Atlanta Falcons in Week 8.

The veteran linebacker played a key role in Tampa Bay's defensive dominance on Sunday, intercepting Falcons quarterback Michael Penix Jr. twice in the first quarter. Anzalone also finished the game with six tackles, including one for loss, helping the Buccaneers secure their second consecutive victory.

Both interceptions came during Atlanta's second and third offensive possessions, setting the tone for a Buccaneers defense that held the Falcons to just 224 total yards and forced four turnovers. Atlanta also failed to convert any of its 12 third-down opportunities.

Anzalone became just the second NFL linebacker since 2000 to record two interceptions in the first quarter of a game, joining former Seattle Seahawks linebacker Lofa Tatupu, who accomplished the feat in 2007.

The recognition marks the first NFC Defensive Player of the Week award of Anzalone's 10-year NFL career and the first weekly award earned by a Buccaneers player this season. He is also the first Tampa Bay defender to receive the honor since outside linebacker Anthony Nelson in Week 8 of the 2025 season.

Signed by Tampa Bay in March after five seasons with the Detroit Lions, Anzalone has recorded 42 tackles, one sack, three interceptions and three passes defensed through eight games this season. His three interceptions lead all NFL linebackers.

The Buccaneers will look to build on their defensive momentum when they travel to Chicago to face the Bears on Sunday Night Football in Week 9.
